@blurb Serve a language model the way production systems do: price every decoding step with a roofline cost model, size and page the KV cache, batch continuously and measure TTFT, TPOT and goodput, decode speculatively without changing the output, quantize weights, cache prefixes and route by them, autoscale replicas, search vectors with an IVF index, and price a token — each built and measured in a simulated server.
@plainvoice true

=== mlsys2-01 | What inference costs: prefill, decode and the KV cache
--- teach
In ML Systems I you priced a training step with the roofline: FLOPs against peak compute, bytes against memory bandwidth, whichever takes longer wins. In the Python AI course you built a transformer block and a bigram model that wrote words one character at a time. Serving puts the two together: a trained model answering many users at once, one token at a time. This lesson prices that work, and builds the small simulated model server that every later lab in this course extends.

### Generating text is a loop

Your bigram sampler picked a next character, appended it, and asked again. A transformer language model generates the same way, with one difference that sets every cost in this course: to choose each next token it runs the **whole model** on the text so far.

A request has two phases.

- **Prefill**: the model reads the prompt. Every prompt token is already known, so all of them go through the model in **one** forward pass, side by side, like a training step without the backward pass.
- **Decode**: the model writes the answer. Token 2 depends on token 1, so each new token needs its own forward pass. A 300-token answer takes about 300 passes, one after another. This is what **[[autoregressive|autoregressive]]** means.

The last prefill pass already produces the first output token: its final position's logits are the prediction for what comes after the prompt. So a request with a 500-token prompt and a 4-token answer takes one prefill pass and 3 decode passes.

### FLOPs per token

In a matrix multiply, each weight is used in one multiply and one add for every token that passes through it. A model with N parameters therefore spends about **2N FLOPs per token**, the **[[2N rule|two-n]]**. A 7-billion-parameter model spends about 14 GFLOP on every token, in prefill and decode alike.

Attention adds work that grows with the context. In each layer, a token compares its query with every earlier key (a dot product over d numbers: 2d FLOPs) and mixes the values (another 2d), so each query–key pair costs about **4d FLOPs per layer**, where d is the model width. A token at position 1,000 of a 32-layer model with d = 4,096 pays 4 · 32 · 4096 · 1000 ≈ 0.52 GFLOP for attention: small next to 14, but it grows with every token.

### Bytes per step

Compute is half the story. A forward pass must bring **every weight** from memory into the chip once. Two bytes per parameter (16-bit weights) means a 7B model moves 14 GB **per step**, whether the step processes one token or a thousand.

That is the whole secret of inference cost:

```python
weights = 14e9          # bytes moved per step, 7B parameters at 2 bytes
flops_per_token = 14e9
for tokens in (1, 1000):
    print(tokens, flops_per_token * tokens / weights)    # FLOPs per byte
# 1 1.0
# 1000 1000.0
```

### The roofline, one token at a time

Recall the roofline: a step takes **time = max(FLOPs / peak, bytes / bandwidth)**, and its **arithmetic intensity** is FLOPs ÷ bytes. The **[[ridge point|ridge]]** is peak ÷ bandwidth: below it a step is **memory-bound** (waiting for bytes), above it **compute-bound**. Take an accelerator with a peak of 10¹⁵ FLOP/s and 3.35 × 10¹² bytes/s of bandwidth: its ridge is about 300 FLOPs per byte.

- **Prefill** of a 1,000-token prompt has intensity about 1,000: compute-bound. It takes about 14 × 10¹² / 10¹⁵ = 14 ms.
- **Decode** for one user has intensity about 1: deeply memory-bound. Each token takes 14 × 10⁹ / 3.35 × 10¹² ≈ 4.2 ms, so one user gets at most about 240 tokens a second, and the compute units sit more than 99% idle.

Now decode for **B** users in the same step. The weights are read once and used B times: about 2N · B FLOPs for the same 2N bytes, so the intensity is about B. Until B nears the ridge, a step for 64 users with short contexts takes barely longer than a step for one. **Batching decode is almost free**, and that one fact drives everything in serving: memory, scheduling, routing and price all come down to keeping big batches of decodes running.

### The KV cache

To pick token t, attention needs the keys and values of all earlier tokens. Those never change, because a causal model never lets an earlier token see a later one. So the server stores them instead of recomputing them on every step: the **KV cache**, the keys and values of every token a request has seen, kept in accelerator memory until the request ends. Without it, step t would redo the work for all t earlier tokens, and an n-token answer would cost about n²/2 token-passes instead of n.

What is stored, per token: for each layer, for each key–value head, one key and one value vector of head_dim numbers.

**KV bytes per token = 2 × layers × KV heads × head_dim × bytes per number**

The 2 is "a key and a value". A model with 32 layers, 8 KV heads of 128 numbers each, in 16-bit, stores 2 · 32 · 8 · 128 · 2 = 131,072 bytes, 128 KiB, for **every token of every request**. Many models use fewer KV heads than query heads, called **[[grouped-query attention|gqa]]**, precisely to shrink this number; with one KV head per query head (32 here) it would be 512 KiB.

Scale it up:

- one request of 4,000 tokens: 4,000 × 128 KiB = 500 MiB;
- a batch of 64 such requests: about 31 GiB;
- an 80 GB accelerator holding 14 GB of weights has about 66 GB left: about 500,000 tokens of cache in total.

The cache also costs **bandwidth**. Each decode step reads every running request's whole cache. At long contexts those reads outgrow the weights: 64 requests × 4,000 tokens × 128 KiB is 33.5 GB per step, more than twice the 14 GB of weights.

### The cost model you will build

The lab turns all of this into one function. A model and an accelerator are described by two small classes:

```python fragment
model = Model(n_params=7_000_000_000, n_layers=32, d_model=4096, n_kv_heads=8, head_dim=128)
hw = Accel(peak_flops=1e15, mem_bw=3.35e12, mem_bytes=80e9)
```

`bytes_per_param` and `kv_bytes` default to 2 (16-bit). One step of the server runs a **batch**: a list of `(n_new, cached)` pairs, one per request in the step. `n_new` is how many tokens of that request go through the model (the whole prompt or a chunk of it in prefill, 1 in decode), and `cached` is how many of its tokens were already in the KV cache. A step that prefills a 300-token prompt and decodes two requests with 80 and 2,000 cached tokens is `[(300, 0), (1, 80), (1, 2000)]`.

Counting the query–key pairs: the i-th new token (i = 1 … n) sees the c cached tokens and the new ones up to itself, c + i keys in all. Adding over i gives **n · c + n(n + 1)/2** pairs for one request. For n = 3 and c = 2: 3 + 4 + 5 = 12, and 3 · 2 + 3 · 4/2 = 12.

The bytes are the weights once, plus each request's cache read and its new entries written: KV bytes per token × (c + n).

### The simulated server

The starter also holds the server, which every later lab reuses unchanged. Time in it is simulated: the clock jumps by each step's cost, so a run is exact and repeatable.

- A `Request` has an id, an `arrival` time, a `prompt_len` and an `output_len`. As it runs it tracks `cached` (tokens in its KV cache), `generated` (output tokens so far) and `token_times` (the clock time each output token came out).
- `make_requests(seed, n, rate)` makes n requests with random lengths. Their arrivals form a **[[Poisson process|poisson]]** at `rate` requests per second, drawn from a seeded `random.Random`, so the same seed gives the same workload.
- `run_server(model, hw, requests, schedule)` runs the loop. Each step it moves arrived requests into a **queue**, asks the **scheduler** for a plan, a list of `(request, n_new)` pairs, prices the plan with `step_cost`, and moves the clock. Then each planned request adds `n_new` to `cached`, and once `cached` reaches `prompt_len` it emits a token at the new clock time. A request that has emitted `output_len` tokens is finished. It returns the finished requests and a log with one entry per step: `(time, requests in the step, tokens in the step, cached tokens held)`.
- `one_at_a_time` is the simplest scheduler: the oldest request alone, its whole prompt in one step, then one decode at a time until it ends. Lesson 3 replaces it with a real one.

**Watch out:**

- **Weights are read once per step, not once per token.** Charging them per token makes batching look useless, which is the opposite of the truth.
- **K and V are two tensors.** Forgetting the 2 halves the cache, and the server runs out of memory at twice the batch you planned.
- **Count KV heads, not query heads.** With grouped-query attention the cache is several times smaller than d_model suggests.
- **GB and GiB differ by 7%.** Memory sizes on spec sheets are usually 10⁹ bytes; a KiB is 1,024 bytes.

::: context autoregressive Each token waits for the one before
An autoregressive model predicts each value from the values before it, then feeds its own prediction back in as input. That loop is why decoding cannot be spread across time the way prefill can: token 51 does not exist until token 50 has been chosen. Everything in this course that speeds decoding up works around the loop rather than breaking it. Batching runs many requests' loops side by side, speculative decoding (lesson 4) guesses several steps ahead and checks them in one pass, and quantization (lesson 5) makes each pass move fewer bytes.
:::

::: context two-n Where 2N comes from
A layer that multiplies a token's vector of length m by a weight matrix of shape m × k does m · k multiplies and about as many adds: 2 FLOPs per weight. Summing over every weight matrix gives 2N per token. The rule leaves out attention's query–key work, the softmax and the normalisations, which are small unless the context is long. Training costs about 6N per token, because the backward pass needs about twice the forward pass's work; ML Systems I used that number. Inference is the forward pass alone.
:::

::: context ridge Reading the roofline for serving
The ridge point is the arithmetic intensity at which memory and compute take the same time. For the accelerator in this lesson it is 10¹⁵ / 3.35 × 10¹² ≈ 300 FLOPs per byte. Decode at batch 1 sits at about 1, far left on the sloped part; prefill sits far right on the flat part.

```svg

  <line x1="40" y1="150" x2="40" y2="15" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

  <text x="220" y="166" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">ridge, about 300</text>
  <circle cx="52" cy="133" r="5" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="60" y="122" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">decode, batch 1</text>
  <circle cx="160" cy="73" r="5" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="150" y="62" font-size="11" text-anchor="end" fill="#1f2a44">decode, batch 64</text>
  <circle cx="300" cy="40" r="5" fill="#1d6fd1"/>

  <text x="14" y="90" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44" transform="rotate(-90 14 90)">FLOP/s</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context gqa Fewer heads for keys and values
In multi-head attention every query head has its own key and value head. Grouped-query attention lets a group of query heads share one key–value head: 32 query heads with 8 KV heads means groups of 4. The cache shrinks by the group size, and so do the bytes each decode step reads, with only a small loss in quality. Multi-query attention is the extreme case, one KV head for all queries. Many open models released since 2023 use grouped-query attention, and some newer designs compress the keys and values further into a small latent vector per token.
:::

::: context poisson Arrivals that do not remember
When many independent users each send a request now and then, the arrivals in total look like a Poisson process: the gap before the next arrival is exponentially distributed, with mean 1/rate, and does not depend on how long you have already waited. `rng.expovariate(rate)` draws such a gap. Real traffic is burstier than Poisson, with daily cycles and sudden spikes, which lesson 7 meets when it autoscales; but Poisson is the standard first model, and it is the one queueing theory's formulas from Cloud Infrastructure assume.
:::
--- task
The starter holds the `Model` and `Accel` classes and the whole simulated server. Write the two functions of the cost model that the server calls.

- `kv_bytes_per_token(model)`: the KV cache bytes one token takes: 2 × `n_layers` × `n_kv_heads` × `head_dim` × `kv_bytes`.
- `step_cost(model, hw, work)`: the cost of one step. `work` is a list of `(n_new, cached)` pairs of whole numbers. Return a dict with four keys:
  - `"flops"`: 2 × `n_params` × (the total of `n_new`) + 4 × `n_layers` × `d_model` × (the total of n · c + n(n + 1)/2 over the pairs, with n = `n_new` and c = `cached`). Use whole-number arithmetic (`//` for the division by 2), so the result is an exact `int`.
  - `"bytes"`: `n_params` × `bytes_per_param` + `kv_bytes_per_token(model)` × (the total of c + n over the pairs).
  - `"time"`: the larger of `flops / hw.peak_flops` and `bytes / hw.mem_bw`, in seconds.
  - `"bound"`: `"compute"` if the compute time is strictly larger than the memory time, otherwise `"memory"`.

An empty `work` list costs nothing: return `{"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}`.

For example, with the 7B model and accelerator from the lesson, `step_cost(model, hw, [(1, 1000)])` has `"flops"` 14,524,812,288, `"bytes"` 14,131,203,072, a time of about 4.218 ms, and is `"memory"` bound.
--- starter


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 0


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}

class Request:
    def __init__(self, rid, arrival, prompt_len, output_len):
        self.rid = rid
        self.arrival = arrival
        self.prompt_len = prompt_len
        self.output_len = output_len
        self.cached = 0
        self.generated = 0
        self.token_times = []

    def done(self):
        return self.generated >= self.output_len


def make_requests(seed, n, rate, prompt=(64, 512), output=(16, 256)):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    t = 0.0
    out = []
    for rid in range(n):
        t += rng.expovariate(rate)
        out.append(Request(rid, round(t, 6), rng.randint(*prompt), rng.randint(*output)))
    return out


def run_server(model, hw, requests, schedule):
    pending = sorted(requests, key=lambda r: (r.arrival, r.rid))
    queue, running, finished, log = [], [], [], []
    now, i = 0.0, 0
    while i < len(pending) or queue or running:
        while i < len(pending) and pending[i].arrival <= now:
            queue.append(pending[i])
            i += 1
        plan = schedule(queue, running, now) if queue or running else []
        if not plan:
            if i < len(pending):
                now = max(now, pending[i].arrival)
                continue
            if queue or running:
                raise RuntimeError("requests are waiting but the scheduler planned nothing")
            break
        for req, n in plan:
            if req in queue:
                queue.remove(req)
                running.append(req)
        now += step_cost(model, hw, [(n, req.cached) for req, n in plan])["time"]
        for req, n in plan:
            req.cached += n
            if req.cached >= req.prompt_len:
                req.generated += 1
                req.token_times.append(now)
        log.append((now, len(plan), sum(n for _, n in plan), sum(r.cached for r in running)))
        for req in [r for r in running if r.done()]:
            running.remove(req)
            finished.append(req)
    return finished, log


def one_at_a_time(queue, running, now):
    if running:
        return [(running[0], 1)]
    if queue:
        return [(queue[0], queue[0].prompt_len)]
    return []


--- solution


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


class Request:
    def __init__(self, rid, arrival, prompt_len, output_len):
        self.rid = rid
        self.arrival = arrival
        self.prompt_len = prompt_len
        self.output_len = output_len
        self.cached = 0
        self.generated = 0
        self.token_times = []

    def done(self):
        return self.generated >= self.output_len


def make_requests(seed, n, rate, prompt=(64, 512), output=(16, 256)):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    t = 0.0
    out = []
    for rid in range(n):
        t += rng.expovariate(rate)
        out.append(Request(rid, round(t, 6), rng.randint(*prompt), rng.randint(*output)))
    return out


def run_server(model, hw, requests, schedule):
    pending = sorted(requests, key=lambda r: (r.arrival, r.rid))
    queue, running, finished, log = [], [], [], []
    now, i = 0.0, 0
    while i < len(pending) or queue or running:
        while i < len(pending) and pending[i].arrival <= now:
            queue.append(pending[i])
            i += 1
        plan = schedule(queue, running, now) if queue or running else []
        if not plan:
            if i < len(pending):
                now = max(now, pending[i].arrival)
                continue
            if queue or running:
                raise RuntimeError("requests are waiting but the scheduler planned nothing")
            break
        for req, n in plan:
            if req in queue:
                queue.remove(req)
                running.append(req)
        now += step_cost(model, hw, [(n, req.cached) for req, n in plan])["time"]
        for req, n in plan:
            req.cached += n
            if req.cached >= req.prompt_len:
                req.generated += 1
                req.token_times.append(now)
        log.append((now, len(plan), sum(n for _, n in plan), sum(r.cached for r in running)))
        for req in [r for r in running if r.done()]:
            running.remove(req)
            finished.append(req)
    return finished, log


def one_at_a_time(queue, running, now):
    if running:
        return [(running[0], 1)]
    if queue:
        return [(queue[0], queue[0].prompt_len)]
    return []


--- hint
`kv_bytes_per_token` is one product. In `step_cost`, add up three totals over the pairs first: the tokens (`n`), the query–key pairs (`n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2`) and the cache tokens touched (`c + n`).
--- hint
FLOPs are `2 * n_params * tokens + 4 * n_layers * d_model * pairs`. Bytes are the weights once (`n_params * bytes_per_param`) plus `kv_bytes_per_token(model) * touched`. Divide each by its rate to get two times.
--- hint
The time is `max(t_compute, t_memory)` and the bound is `"compute"` only when `t_compute > t_memory`. Handle the empty list first, before any division.
--- check case | KV bytes per token for the lesson's model, and for one with 32 KV heads in 8-bit
(kv_bytes_per_token(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128)), kv_bytes_per_token(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 32, 128, kv_bytes=1)))
=> (131072, 262144)
--- check case | One decode at 1,000 cached tokens: exact FLOPs and bytes
(lambda c: (c["flops"], c["bytes"], c["bound"]))(step_cost(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), [(1, 1000)]))
=> (14524812288, 14131203072, "memory")
--- check test | Its time is the memory time, about 4.218 ms
abs(step_cost(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), [(1, 1000)])["time"] - 14131203072 / 3.35e12) < 1e-12
--- check case | Query–key pairs: n·c + n(n + 1)/2 for each request
(lambda m, hw: [step_cost(m, hw, w)["flops"] for w in ([(4, 0)], [(3, 5)], [(4, 0), (3, 5)])])(Model(10, 1, 1, 1, 1), Accel(1, 1, 1))
=> [120, 144, 264]
?? With 10 parameters, 1 layer and width 1, a step costs 20 FLOPs per token plus 4 per query–key pair. (4, 0) has 10 pairs; (3, 5) has 15 + 6 = 21.
--- check case | A 1,000-token prefill is compute-bound
(lambda c: (c["flops"], c["bytes"], c["bound"], round(c["time"], 9)))(step_cost(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), [(1000, 0)]))
=> (14262406144000, 14131072000, 'compute', 0.014262406)
--- check case | The empty batch costs nothing
step_cost(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), [])
=> {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
--- check case | A tie between compute and memory counts as memory-bound
step_cost(Model(10, 1, 1, 1, 1, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2), Accel(24, 24, 1), [(1, 0)])
=> {"flops": 24, "bytes": 24, "time": 1.0, "bound": "memory"}
--- check test | Batching decode: 64 users cost far less than 64 times one user
(lambda m, hw: step_cost(m, hw, [(1, 500)] * 64)["time"] < 1.5 * step_cost(m, hw, [(1, 500)])["time"])(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9))
--- check case | The server, one request at a time: when each first token comes out
(lambda f: [(r.rid, round(r.token_times[0] - r.arrival, 6), round(r.token_times[-1], 6)) for r in f])(run_server(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), [Request(0, 0.0, 1000, 3), Request(1, 0.001, 200, 2)], one_at_a_time)[0])
=> [(0, 0.014262, 0.022699), (1, 0.025886, 0.031073)]
?? The second request waits for the whole first one: its time to first token includes the first request's prefill and both of its decode steps.

+++ question | Why one user leaves the chip idle
--- ask
Decoding for a single user, a 7B model on an accelerator with a ridge point near 300 FLOPs per byte uses under 1% of its peak compute. Why?
--- choice
The decode step's matrix multiplies are too small to fit the accelerator's cores.
--- choice correct
Each step must read all 14 GB of weights to do only about 14 GFLOP of work: about 1 FLOP per byte, so the step waits on memory.
--- choice
Attention over the KV cache dominates the step, and it cannot run in parallel.
--- choice
The accelerator must wait for the sampler to pick each token on the CPU.
--- why
Arithmetic intensity, not matrix size, decides. One token does 2N FLOPs against 2N bytes of weights, intensity 1, about 300 times below the ridge. Batching B users raises the intensity to about B with almost no extra time, which is why servers batch.

+++ question | Cache per token
--- ask
A model has 40 layers and 8 key–value heads of 128 numbers each, stored in 16-bit. How many KiB of KV cache does one token take? Type a whole number.
--- answer
160
160 KiB
--- why
2 × 40 × 8 × 128 × 2 bytes = 163,840 bytes, and 163,840 / 1,024 = 160 KiB. The first 2 is a key and a value; the last is two bytes per number.

+++ question | Doubling the batch
--- ask
A server decodes 8 requests per step, each with 200 cached tokens, on the 7B model and accelerator from this lesson. It doubles the batch to 16. What happens to the time of one step?
--- choice
It doubles, because twice the tokens need twice the work.
--- choice correct
It grows only a little, because the step is memory-bound and the 14 GB of weights are still read once.
--- choice
It halves, because the work is spread over more requests.
--- choice
It stays exactly the same, because the KV cache is not read during decode.
--- why
At this size the step's time is set by bytes, and the weights dominate them. Eight more requests add only their small caches (16 × 200 × 128 KiB is about 0.4 GB) to the 14 GB. The throughput nearly doubles for free.

+++ practice | The batch where decode turns compute-bound
--- task
The starter has the cost model from the lesson. Write `saturating_batch(model, hw, ctx, max_batch)`: the smallest batch size B from 1 to `max_batch` for which a step of B decodes, each with `ctx` cached tokens (the work list `[(1, ctx)] * B`), is `"compute"` bound according to `step_cost`. Return `None` if no B up to `max_batch` is.

The bound only changes once as B grows (more requests only raise the step's FLOPs per byte), so a binary search finds B quickly; trying every B from 1 is too slow for the checks, which use `max_batch` up to 100,000.

For example, a 7B model on the lesson's accelerator with 100 cached tokens per request turns compute-bound at B = 414. At 1,000 cached tokens it never does: each extra request brings its own cache to read, so the FLOPs per byte level off below the ridge.
--- starter


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def saturating_batch(model, hw, ctx, max_batch):
    return 1
--- solution


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def saturating_batch(model, hw, ctx, max_batch):
    def compute_bound(b):
        return step_cost(model, hw, [(1, ctx)] * b)["bound"] == "compute"

    if not compute_bound(max_batch):
        return None
    lo, hi = 1, max_batch
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        if compute_bound(mid):
            hi = mid
        else:
            lo = mid + 1
    return lo
--- hint
Write a helper `compute_bound(b)` that builds `[(1, ctx)] * b` and asks `step_cost` for its bound. If even `max_batch` is not compute-bound, the answer is `None`.
--- hint
Otherwise binary search for the first `b` where the helper is true: keep `lo = 1, hi = max_batch`; if `compute_bound(mid)` holds, the answer is at most `mid`.
--- check case | The example: 100 cached tokens
saturating_batch(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 100, 100000)
=> 414
--- check case | Long contexts never get there
saturating_batch(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, 100000)
=> None
--- check case | Capped by max_batch
saturating_batch(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 100, 300)
=> None
--- check case | A slow chip saturates at batch 1
saturating_batch(Model(1_000_000, 4, 256, 4, 64), Accel(1e6, 1e9, 1e9), 10, 50)
=> 1
--- check case | 8-bit weights halve the bytes, so compute-bound comes sooner
saturating_batch(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128, bytes_per_param=1), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 100, 100000)
=> 207
--- check case | A model without grouped-query attention, short context
saturating_batch(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 32, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 20, 100000)
=> 390

+++ practice | How many requests fit in memory
--- task
Write two functions about what fits in accelerator memory. Memory holds the weights (`n_params` × `bytes_per_param` bytes) and the KV cache; nothing else.

- `max_sequences(model, hw, ctx)`: how many requests of `ctx` tokens each can have their whole KV cache in memory at once, as a whole number (round down). Return 0 if the weights alone do not fit.
- `max_context(model, hw, batch)`: the longest context, in whole tokens (round down), that `batch` requests can each hold at once. Return 0 if the weights alone do not fit.

`kv_bytes_per_token(model)` from the starter gives the bytes per token. `ctx` and `batch` are at least 1.

For example, the lesson's 7B model on an 80 GB accelerator leaves 66 × 10⁹ bytes for the cache, so `max_sequences(model, hw, 4000)` is 125 and `max_context(model, hw, 64)` is 7,867.
--- starter

import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def free_for_cache(model, hw):
    return hw.mem_bytes - model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param


def max_sequences(model, hw, ctx):
    free = free_for_cache(model, hw)
    if free <= 0:
        return 0
    return int(free // (kv_bytes_per_token(model) * ctx))


def max_context(model, hw, batch):
    free = free_for_cache(model, hw)
    if free <= 0:
        return 0
    return int(free // (kv_bytes_per_token(model) * batch))
--- hint
First work out the bytes left for the cache: memory minus the weights. If that is 0 or less, nothing fits.
--- hint
One request of `ctx` tokens needs `kv_bytes_per_token(model) * ctx` bytes. Floor-divide the free bytes by that, and wrap it in `int(...)`, because the memory size may be a float such as `80e9`.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(max_sequences(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 4000), max_context(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 64))
=> (125, 7867)
--- check case | Without grouped-query attention, four times fewer
max_sequences(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 32, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 4000)
=> 31
--- check case | Weights that do not fit leave nothing
(max_sequences(Model(70_000_000_000, 80, 8192, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 100), max_context(Model(70_000_000_000, 80, 8192, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1))
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | Weights that fill memory exactly leave nothing
max_sequences(Model(40_000_000_000, 48, 6144, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1)
=> 0
--- check case | Results are whole numbers, rounded down
(lambda a, b: (type(a).__name__, type(b).__name__, a, b))(max_sequences(Model(1_000_000_000, 16, 2048, 4, 128), Accel(1e14, 1e12, 24e9), 3000), max_context(Model(1_000_000_000, 16, 2048, 4, 128), Accel(1e14, 1e12, 24e9), 7))
=> ('int', 'int', 223, 95912)

+++ practice | Decode speed for one user and for many
--- task
Write `decode_rates(model, hw, ctx, batches)`. For each batch size B in the list `batches`, price one decode step of B requests, each with `ctx` cached tokens (`[(1, ctx)] * B`), with `step_cost`. Return a list of tuples `(B, per_user, total, bound)`:

- `per_user`: tokens per second each user sees, 1 / step time, rounded to 1 decimal place;
- `total`: tokens per second the server produces, B / step time, rounded to 1 decimal place;
- `bound`: the step's `"bound"`.

For example, for the lesson's 7B model and accelerator at `ctx` 1000, `decode_rates(model, hw, 1000, [1])` is `[(1, 237.1, 237.1, "memory")]`.
--- starter

--- solution


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def decode_rates(model, hw, ctx, batches):
    out = []
    for b in batches:
        cost = step_cost(model, hw, [(1, ctx)] * b)
        out.append((b, round(1 / cost["time"], 1), round(b / cost["time"], 1), cost["bound"]))
    return out
--- hint
One call to `step_cost` per batch size. A step produces one token for each of its B requests, and takes `cost["time"]` seconds.
--- check case | The example from the task
decode_rates(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, [1])
=> [(1, 237.1, 237.1, "memory")]
--- check case | Batching multiplies the total, while each user slows only a little
decode_rates(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, [1, 8, 64, 256])
=> [(1, 237.1, 237.1, 'memory'), (8, 222.6, 1780.8, 'memory'), (64, 149.6, 9572.7, 'memory'), (256, 70.4, 18021.4, 'memory')]
--- check case | Short contexts reach the compute roof
decode_rates(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 50, [512, 1024])
=> [(512, 139.2, 71292.4, 'compute'), (1024, 69.6, 71292.4, 'compute')]
--- check case | An empty list of batch sizes
decode_rates(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, [])
=> []

+++ practice | One request, alone on the server
--- task
Write `solo_latency(model, hw, prompt_len, output_len)` for a request that has the server to itself. It returns `(ttft, total)`: the seconds until its first output token, and until its last.

Follow the lesson's model of a request. The first step prefills the whole prompt, `[(prompt_len, 0)]`, and that step already emits output token 1. Every later output token takes one decode step of the form `[(1, cached)]`, where `cached` is the number of tokens in the cache before the step: `prompt_len` for token 2, `prompt_len + 1` for token 3, and so on. Price each step with `step_cost`. Round both results to 9 decimal places.

`output_len` is at least 1; with 1, there is no decode step and `total` equals `ttft`. Your answer must agree with what `run_server` does with the `one_at_a_time` scheduler.
--- starter


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def solo_latency(model, hw, prompt_len, output_len):
    t = step_cost(model, hw, [(prompt_len, 0)])["time"]
    return (round(t, 9), round(t * output_len, 9))

class Request:
    def __init__(self, rid, arrival, prompt_len, output_len):
        self.rid = rid
        self.arrival = arrival
        self.prompt_len = prompt_len
        self.output_len = output_len
        self.cached = 0
        self.generated = 0
        self.token_times = []

    def done(self):
        return self.generated >= self.output_len


def make_requests(seed, n, rate, prompt=(64, 512), output=(16, 256)):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    t = 0.0
    out = []
    for rid in range(n):
        t += rng.expovariate(rate)
        out.append(Request(rid, round(t, 6), rng.randint(*prompt), rng.randint(*output)))
    return out


def run_server(model, hw, requests, schedule):
    pending = sorted(requests, key=lambda r: (r.arrival, r.rid))
    queue, running, finished, log = [], [], [], []
    now, i = 0.0, 0
    while i < len(pending) or queue or running:
        while i < len(pending) and pending[i].arrival <= now:
            queue.append(pending[i])
            i += 1
        plan = schedule(queue, running, now) if queue or running else []
        if not plan:
            if i < len(pending):
                now = max(now, pending[i].arrival)
                continue
            if queue or running:
                raise RuntimeError("requests are waiting but the scheduler planned nothing")
            break
        for req, n in plan:
            if req in queue:
                queue.remove(req)
                running.append(req)
        now += step_cost(model, hw, [(n, req.cached) for req, n in plan])["time"]
        for req, n in plan:
            req.cached += n
            if req.cached >= req.prompt_len:
                req.generated += 1
                req.token_times.append(now)
        log.append((now, len(plan), sum(n for _, n in plan), sum(r.cached for r in running)))
        for req in [r for r in running if r.done()]:
            running.remove(req)
            finished.append(req)
    return finished, log


def one_at_a_time(queue, running, now):
    if running:
        return [(running[0], 1)]
    if queue:
        return [(queue[0], queue[0].prompt_len)]
    return []


--- solution

import random

        self.n_layers = n_layers

        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def solo_latency(model, hw, prompt_len, output_len):
    ttft = step_cost(model, hw, [(prompt_len, 0)])["time"]
    total = ttft
    cached = prompt_len
    for _ in range(output_len - 1):
        total += step_cost(model, hw, [(1, cached)])["time"]
        cached += 1
    return (round(ttft, 9), round(total, 9))

class Request:
    def __init__(self, rid, arrival, prompt_len, output_len):
        self.rid = rid
        self.arrival = arrival
        self.prompt_len = prompt_len
        self.output_len = output_len
        self.cached = 0
        self.generated = 0
        self.token_times = []

    def done(self):
        return self.generated >= self.output_len


def make_requests(seed, n, rate, prompt=(64, 512), output=(16, 256)):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    t = 0.0
    out = []
    for rid in range(n):
        t += rng.expovariate(rate)
        out.append(Request(rid, round(t, 6), rng.randint(*prompt), rng.randint(*output)))
    return out


def run_server(model, hw, requests, schedule):
    pending = sorted(requests, key=lambda r: (r.arrival, r.rid))
    queue, running, finished, log = [], [], [], []
    now, i = 0.0, 0
    while i < len(pending) or queue or running:
        while i < len(pending) and pending[i].arrival <= now:
            queue.append(pending[i])
            i += 1
        plan = schedule(queue, running, now) if queue or running else []
        if not plan:
            if i < len(pending):
                now = max(now, pending[i].arrival)
                continue
            if queue or running:
                raise RuntimeError("requests are waiting but the scheduler planned nothing")
            break
        for req, n in plan:
            if req in queue:
                queue.remove(req)
                running.append(req)
        now += step_cost(model, hw, [(n, req.cached) for req, n in plan])["time"]
        for req, n in plan:
            req.cached += n
            if req.cached >= req.prompt_len:
                req.generated += 1
                req.token_times.append(now)
        log.append((now, len(plan), sum(n for _, n in plan), sum(r.cached for r in running)))
        for req in [r for r in running if r.done()]:
            running.remove(req)
            finished.append(req)
    return finished, log


def one_at_a_time(queue, running, now):
    if running:
        return [(running[0], 1)]
    if queue:
        return [(queue[0], queue[0].prompt_len)]
    return []


--- hint
Keep a running `total` that starts at the prefill time, and a `cached` count that starts at `prompt_len`. There are `output_len - 1` decode steps.
--- hint
Each decode step costs `step_cost(model, hw, [(1, cached)])["time"]`, and adds one token to the cache afterwards, so the next step sees one more.
--- check case | A 1,000-token prompt and a 3-token answer
solo_latency(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, 3)
=> (0.014262406, 0.022698984)
--- check case | One output token: no decode at all
solo_latency(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 512, 1)
=> (0.007236854, 0.007236854)
--- check case | A long answer: decode dominates
solo_latency(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 200, 2000)
=> (0.00418693, 8.452072158)
--- check test | Agrees with the simulated server
(lambda m, hw: (lambda r: solo_latency(m, hw, 300, 40) == (round(r.token_times[0] - r.arrival, 9), round(r.token_times[-1] - r.arrival, 9)))(run_server(m, hw, [Request(0, 0.0, 300, 40)], one_at_a_time)[0][0]))(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9))
--- check test | Fast even for 50,000 tokens
solo_latency(Model(1_000_000_000, 16, 2048, 4, 128), Accel(1e14, 1e12, 24e9), 100, 50000)[1] > 50

+++ practice | Debug: the cache that is four times too big
--- task
`kv_cache_gib(model, seq_lens)` should return the KV cache, in GiB (2³⁰ bytes), that requests with the token counts in `seq_lens` take together, rounded to 3 decimal places. It was written for an older model where every query head had its own key–value head, and it gave the right answers there. On the lesson's model, which has 32 query heads but only 8 KV heads, it reports four times too much: for one request of 8,192 tokens it says 4.0 GiB instead of 1.0. Find the bug and fix it. An empty list takes 0.0 GiB.
--- starter


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_cache_gib(model, seq_lens):
    per_token = 2 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * model.kv_bytes
    return round(per_token * sum(seq_lens) / 2**30, 3)
--- solution


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_cache_gib(model, seq_lens):
    per_token = 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes
    return round(per_token * sum(seq_lens) / 2**30, 3)
--- hint
`d_model` is the number of query heads times `head_dim`. Which heads does the cache store keys and values for?
--- hint
The cache holds one key and one value per **KV head**: replace `d_model` with `n_kv_heads * head_dim`. On the old model the two were equal, which is why the bug hid.
--- check case | The example from the task
kv_cache_gib(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), [8192])
=> 1.0
--- check case | Several requests add up
kv_cache_gib(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), [4000, 1000, 3192])
=> 1.0
--- check case | The old model, with one KV head per query head, is unchanged
kv_cache_gib(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 32, 128), [8192])
=> 4.0
--- check case | 8-bit cache and an empty list
(kv_cache_gib(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128, kv_bytes=1), [8192, 8192]), kv_cache_gib(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), []))
=> (1.0, 0.0)

+++ practice | Choosing the number of KV heads
--- task
You are designing a model's attention. It has `n_heads` query heads, and the number of key–value heads must divide `n_heads` evenly (each group of query heads shares one KV head). More KV heads give better quality but a bigger cache.

Write `best_kv_heads(model, hw, n_heads, batch, ctx)`: the **largest** divisor h of `n_heads` such that, with `n_kv_heads = h`, the weights plus the KV cache of `batch` requests of `ctx` tokens each fit in `hw.mem_bytes` (fitting exactly counts). Return `None` if not even h = 1 fits. Use the `model`'s other fields as they are; build a new `Model` (or compute directly) for each h rather than changing `model`.

For example, the lesson's 7B model (32 query heads) serving 64 requests of 8,000 tokens on an 80 GB accelerator: h = 8 needs 64 × 8,000 × 128 KiB ≈ 67.1 GB of cache, which with 14 GB of weights is too much, while h = 4 needs half. So the answer is 4.
--- starter

import random

        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)

    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}

    return n_heads
--- solution


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def best_kv_heads(model, hw, n_heads, batch, ctx):
    weights = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param
    for h in range(n_heads, 0, -1):
        if n_heads % h:
            continue
        trial = Model(model.n_params, model.n_layers, model.d_model, h, model.head_dim,
                      model.bytes_per_param, model.kv_bytes)
        if weights + kv_bytes_per_token(trial) * batch * ctx <= hw.mem_bytes:
            return h
    return None
--- hint
Try the divisors of `n_heads` from the largest down, and return the first that fits. `n_heads % h == 0` tests a divisor.
--- hint
For each h, the memory needed is `n_params * bytes_per_param + 2 * n_layers * h * head_dim * kv_bytes * batch * ctx`. Compare it with `hw.mem_bytes` using `<=`.
--- check case | The example from the task
best_kv_heads(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 32, 64, 8000)
=> 4
--- check case | Plenty of room: every head gets its own KV head
best_kv_heads(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 32, 8, 2000)
=> 32
--- check case | Only divisors count: 12 heads allow 12, 6, 4, 3, 2 or 1
best_kv_heads(Model(1_000_000_000, 24, 1536, 12, 128), Accel(1e14, 1e12, 24e9), 12, 64, 4000)
=> 6
--- check case | Nothing fits, not even one KV head
best_kv_heads(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 32, 1000, 100000)
=> None
--- check case | Fitting exactly counts
best_kv_heads(Model(1000, 1, 8, 1, 4, bytes_per_param=1, kv_bytes=1), Accel(1, 1, 1128), 2, 2, 4)
=> 2
--- check test | The model passed in is not changed
(lambda m: (best_kv_heads(m, Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 32, 64, 8000), m.n_kv_heads) == (4, 8))(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128))

=== mlsys2-02 | Paged KV memory: block tables and copy-on-write
--- teach
Last lesson showed that a server earns its throughput by running big batches of decodes, and that every running request holds a KV cache that grows by one token per step: 128 KiB a token for the 7B model. So the batch a server can run is set by how many caches fit in memory. This lesson is about not wasting that memory. You will build the allocator that modern servers use: the KV cache split into fixed-size **pages**, the same idea your Operating Systems course used for virtual memory.

### The problem: lengths you cannot know

When a request arrives, the server knows its prompt length but not its answer length. The model decides when to stop. The simplest design reserves, for each request, one **contiguous** region big enough for the longest answer allowed: prompt plus, say, 2,048 tokens.

Most answers are much shorter than the limit. A request that stops after 150 tokens leaves 1,898 reserved tokens unused for its whole life. That is **internal fragmentation**: memory reserved inside an allocation but never used. When requests of different sizes come and go, free memory also breaks into holes between regions, none big enough for the next request even though their total is: **external fragmentation**. Early serving systems measured that only 20 to 40% of their KV memory held real tokens. The batch, and so the throughput, was two to five times smaller than the memory allowed.

### Pages for tokens

Operating Systems solved the same problem for processes. Memory is cut into fixed-size **pages**, a process's addresses map to pages through a **page table**, and a process gets a new page only when it touches one. Pages can sit anywhere in physical memory.

The **paged KV cache** does exactly this:

- the cache memory is cut into **blocks**, each holding the keys and values of **block_size** tokens (16 is a common choice);
- each sequence has a **[[block table|page-table]]**: a list whose entry i is the physical block that holds its tokens i · block_size to (i + 1) · block_size − 1;
- a sequence gets a new block only when its last block is full.

The attention kernel reads a sequence's keys through its block table, so the blocks need not be next to each other. A sequence of L tokens holds ⌈L / block_size⌉ blocks, read "the ceiling of L over block_size", the division rounded up. In Python, `-(-a // b)` is that rounded-up division for whole numbers:

```python
block_size = 16
for tokens in (1, 16, 17, 40):
    print(tokens, -(-tokens // block_size))
# 1 1
# 16 1
# 17 2
# 40 3
```

The waste per sequence is now only the unused part of its **last** block: at most block_size − 1 tokens, about half a block on average. External fragmentation disappears, because every block is the same size: any free block serves any sequence.

### The allocator

The allocator keeps a **free list** of physical blocks. Three operations matter.

- **Grow**: before a step writes n more tokens of a sequence, make room. If the last block has space, use it; take new blocks from the free list for the rest.
- **Free**: when a request finishes, its blocks go back on the free list.
- **All or nothing**: if there are not enough free blocks for the whole growth, change nothing and report failure, so the scheduler can decide what to do (lesson 3 and this lesson's practice). A half-done growth would leave a sequence whose table and length disagree.

Two sequences that grow in turn end up with interleaved blocks, which is fine:

| step | grows | table of A | table of B |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A by 16 tokens | [0] | |
| 2 | B by 16 tokens | [0] | [1] |
| 3 | A by 1 token | [0, 2] | [1] |

### Sharing, and copy-on-write

Paging gives a second gift: two sequences can point at the **same** physical block. That happens all the time.

- **Parallel sampling**: a user asks for 4 different answers to one prompt. All 4 share the prompt's cache.
- **Beam search** keeps several candidate continuations of one prefix.
- **Shared system prompts**: thousands of requests start with the same instructions (lesson 6 builds a cache for that).

A **fork** gives a new sequence a copy of another's block table, not of its blocks. Each block carries a **reference count**: how many block tables contain it. A block goes back on the free list only when its count drops to 0.

A shared block must never be written, or one sequence's new token would appear in the other's cache. Only the **last** block of a sequence is ever written, and only while it is not full. So the rule is **[[copy-on-write|cow]]**: before writing into a last block whose count is above 1, take a fresh block, copy the shared one's contents into it, point this sequence's table at the copy, and lower the old block's count by 1. Full blocks are never written again, so they stay shared for good, at no cost.

Picture a prompt of 40 tokens with block_size 16, forked into two samples: blocks [0, 1, 2], where block 2 holds tokens 32 to 39 and is half full. The first sample to write a token copies block 2, say to block 3: its table becomes [0, 1, 3]. Now block 2 has a count of 1, so the other sample writes into it directly. Two full blocks stayed shared, and one copy was made.

### The invariants

The allocator is right when these always hold, and the lab's checks test them after random sequences of operations:

1. Every block's reference count equals the number of block tables that contain it.
2. The free list holds exactly the blocks with count 0, each once.
3. A sequence of length L has ⌈L / block_size⌉ blocks in its table.

### What it costs

Every operation is cheap: growing by n tokens takes O(1 + n / block_size) blocks, a fork costs O(blocks in the table), a free the same. The choice of block size is a trade-off. Smaller blocks waste less memory, but make block tables longer, and the attention kernel reads each block separately, so very small blocks read memory less efficiently. Servers use 8 to 32 tokens.

**[[Preemption|preempt]]** is the fallback when memory runs out mid-flight: running sequences grow every step, so even a careful scheduler can find no free block. It then evicts a sequence, frees its blocks, and redoes its work later.

**Watch out:**

- **Freeing a shared block.** Free must lower the count and return the block only at 0. Returning it while another table still points at it hands the same memory to two sequences, which corrupts both caches silently.
- **Copying full blocks.** Only a partly filled last block needs copy-on-write. Copying full shared blocks wastes exactly the memory sharing was meant to save.
- **Partial growth.** Check that the whole growth fits before taking any block.
- **Off by one in the block count.** 32 tokens with block_size 16 is 2 blocks, not 3; 33 tokens is 3.

::: context page-table The same picture as virtual memory
Each sequence's block table maps its logical blocks, in order, to physical blocks anywhere in the pool. A shared block appears in two tables and has a reference count of 2.

```svg

  <text x="315" y="91" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">free</text>
  <text x="210" y="122" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">blocks 0 and 1: count 2</text>
  <line x1="75" y1="39" x2="195" y2="72" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
```
