This `speculative_step` follows the lesson's procedure, and its steps have the right lengths, but the text it produces is subtly wrong: over many runs, tokens the draft model likes come out more often than the target model would produce them. For the starter's target and draft, the first token should be `"a"` half the time; this version gives about 0.40. Find the bug and fix it. The starter's `sample`, target and draft models, and `residual` are correct.
--- starter


import random


def sample(dist, rng):
    """Draws one token from a dict {token: probability}, walking the tokens in sorted order."""
    u = rng.random()
    total = 0.0
    last = None
    for token in sorted(dist):
        if dist[token] > 0:
            total += dist[token]
            last = token
            if u < total:
                return token
    return last


# A tiny target model and a cheaper, less accurate draft model: bigram tables,
# so each next-token distribution depends on the last token ("." at the start).
TARGET_TABLE = {
    ".": {"a": 0.5, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.2},
    "a": {"a": 0.1, "b": 0.6, "c": 0.3},
    "b": {"a": 0.7, "b": 0.1, "c": 0.2},
    "c": {"a": 0.3, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.4},
}
DRAFT_TABLE = {
    ".": {"a": 0.3, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.4},
    "a": {"a": 0.2, "b": 0.7, "c": 0.1},
    "b": {"a": 0.4, "b": 0.4, "c": 0.2},
    "c": {"a": 0.6, "b": 0.2, "c": 0.2},
}


def target(prefix):
    return TARGET_TABLE[prefix[-1] if prefix else "."]


def draft(prefix):
    return DRAFT_TABLE[prefix[-1] if prefix else "."]


def residual(p, q):
    diff = {t: max(0.0, pt - q.get(t, 0.0)) for t, pt in p.items()}
    total = sum(diff.values())
    if total == 0:
        return dict(p)
    return {t: v / total for t, v in diff.items() if v > 0}


def speculative_step(target, draft, prefix, k, rng):
    prefix = tuple(prefix)
    guesses = []
    ctx = prefix
    for _ in range(k):
        q = draft(ctx)
        x = sample(q, rng)
        guesses.append((x, q))
        ctx = ctx + (x,)
    out = []
    ctx = prefix
    for x, q in guesses:
        p = target(ctx)
        if rng.random() < p.get(x, 0.0) / q[x]:
            out.append(x)
            ctx = ctx + (x,)
        else:
            out.append(sample(p, rng))
            return out
    out.append(sample(target(ctx), rng))
    return out
--- solution


import random


def sample(dist, rng):
    """Draws one token from a dict {token: probability}, walking the tokens in sorted order."""
    u = rng.random()
    total = 0.0
    last = None
    for token in sorted(dist):
        if dist[token] > 0:
            total += dist[token]
            last = token
            if u < total:
                return token
    return last


# A tiny target model and a cheaper, less accurate draft model: bigram tables,
# so each next-token distribution depends on the last token ("." at the start).
TARGET_TABLE = {
    ".": {"a": 0.5, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.2},
    "a": {"a": 0.1, "b": 0.6, "c": 0.3},
    "b": {"a": 0.7, "b": 0.1, "c": 0.2},
    "c": {"a": 0.3, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.4},
}
DRAFT_TABLE = {
    ".": {"a": 0.3, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.4},
    "a": {"a": 0.2, "b": 0.7, "c": 0.1},
    "b": {"a": 0.4, "b": 0.4, "c": 0.2},
    "c": {"a": 0.6, "b": 0.2, "c": 0.2},
}


def target(prefix):
    return TARGET_TABLE[prefix[-1] if prefix else "."]


def draft(prefix):
    return DRAFT_TABLE[prefix[-1] if prefix else "."]


def residual(p, q):
    diff = {t: max(0.0, pt - q.get(t, 0.0)) for t, pt in p.items()}
    total = sum(diff.values())
    if total == 0:
        return dict(p)
    return {t: v / total for t, v in diff.items() if v > 0}


def speculative_step(target, draft, prefix, k, rng):
    prefix = tuple(prefix)
    guesses = []
    ctx = prefix
    for _ in range(k):
        q = draft(ctx)
        x = sample(q, rng)
        guesses.append((x, q))
        ctx = ctx + (x,)
    out = []
    ctx = prefix
    for x, q in guesses:
        p = target(ctx)
        if rng.random() < p.get(x, 0.0) / q[x]:
            out.append(x)
            ctx = ctx + (x,)
        else:
            out.append(sample(residual(p, q), rng))
            return out
    out.append(sample(target(ctx), rng))
    return out
--- hint
Accepted guesses already give each token min(p, q) of its probability. What should a rejection add, so that the total is p?
--- hint
After a rejection, draw from `residual(p, q)`, not from `p`. Drawing from p gives the min(p, q) part a second time.
--- check test | The first token now matches the target (20,000 steps)
(lambda rng: (lambda c: all(abs(c[t] / 20000 - p) < 0.015 for t, p in TARGET_TABLE["."].items()))(__import__("collections").Counter(speculative_step(target, draft, (), 2, rng)[0] for _ in range(20000))))(__import__("random").Random(21))
--- check test | With fixed p and q the output is exactly p
(lambda rng: (lambda c: abs(c["a"] / 20000 - 0.6) < 0.015 and abs(c["c"] / 20000 - 0.1) < 0.015)(__import__("collections").Counter(speculative_step(lambda pre: {"a": 0.6, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.1}, lambda pre: {"a": 0.2, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.5}, (), 3, rng)[0] for _ in range(20000))))(__import__("random").Random(22))
--- check test | A perfect draft still gives k + 1 tokens
(lambda rng: all(len(speculative_step(target, target, (), 3, rng)) == 4 for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(23))

+++ practice | Choosing a draft model
--- task
A team can deploy one of several draft models for a target. For one typical position, the target's next-token distribution is `p`, and draft i's is `drafts[i][0]`, a dict like `p`; running draft i costs `drafts[i][1]` target passes.

Write `pick_draft(p, drafts, k)`. For each draft, compute its acceptance rate alpha = Σ min(p(t), q(t)) over all tokens (a token missing from a dict has probability 0), then its speedup with `k` guesses: (1 − alpha^(k+1)) / (1 − alpha) divided by (1 + k · cost), using k + 1 for the expected tokens when alpha is 1 (allowing 1e-12 of rounding). Return a pair `(best_index, alphas)`: the index of the draft with the largest speedup (the smaller index on a tie), and the list of alphas rounded to 4 decimal places. If `drafts` is empty, return `(None, [])`.
--- starter
def pick_draft(p, drafts, k):
    return (0, [])
--- solution
def acceptance(p, q):
    return sum(min(p.get(t, 0.0), q.get(t, 0.0)) for t in set(p) | set(q))


def pick_draft(p, drafts, k):
    alphas, speeds = [], []
    for q, cost in drafts:
        a = acceptance(p, q)
        tokens = k + 1 if abs(a - 1) < 1e-12 else (1 - a ** (k + 1)) / (1 - a)
        alphas.append(round(a, 4))
        speeds.append(tokens / (1 + k * cost))
    if not drafts:
        return (None, [])
    best = max(range(len(speeds)), key=lambda i: (speeds[i], -i))
    return (best, alphas)
--- hint
The acceptance rate is the total of `min(p.get(t, 0.0), q.get(t, 0.0))` over every token in either dict: the overlap of the two distributions.
--- hint
Compute each draft's speedup with the lesson's formula, then pick the largest, breaking ties towards the smaller index.
--- check case | A cheap, rough draft against a better, dearer one
pick_draft({"a": 0.5, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.2}, [({"a": 0.3, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.4}, 0.02), ({"a": 0.5, "b": 0.25, "c": 0.25}, 0.15)], 4)
=> (0, [0.8, 0.95])
--- check case | With more guesses the better draft wins
pick_draft({"a": 0.5, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.2}, [({"a": 0.3, "b": 0.3, "c": 0.4}, 0.02), ({"a": 0.5, "b": 0.25, "c": 0.25}, 0.03)], 6)
=> (1, [0.8, 0.95])
--- check case | A draft with disjoint tokens is never accepted
pick_draft({"a": 1.0}, [({"z": 1.0}, 0.01), ({"a": 0.5, "z": 0.5}, 0.01)], 3)
=> (1, [0.0, 0.5])
--- check case | A perfect draft
pick_draft({"a": 0.7, "b": 0.3}, [({"a": 0.7, "b": 0.3}, 0.1)], 5)
=> (0, [1.0])
--- check case | No drafts
pick_draft({"a": 1.0}, [], 3)
=> (None, [])

=== mlsys2-05 | Quantization: fewer bits per weight, error measured
--- teach
Every lesson so far has come back to one number: decode reads all the weights on every step, 14 GB for a 7B model in 16-bit. Speculative decoding got more tokens out of each read. This lesson makes the read itself smaller. Store each weight in 4 bits instead of 16 and a memory-bound step moves about a quarter of the bytes, so it runs nearly four times faster, and the memory saved holds more KV cache. The price is a small error in every weight, and the craft is keeping that error small. In ML Systems I you simulated fp16, bf16 and fp8 bit by bit; here the formats are plain integers with a scale.

### A ruler with few marks

Picture measuring lengths with a ruler that has only 15 marks. You choose where the marks go: spread them over 0 to 15 cm and every length is off by at most half a centimetre; spread them over 0 to 150 cm and the error is ten times worse. Integer quantization is that ruler.

**Symmetric quantization** with b bits stores each weight w as a small integer q and keeps one real number, the **scale** s, for a whole group of weights:

- the integers run from −qmax to qmax, where **qmax = 2^(b−1) − 1**: 127 for int8, 7 for int4;
- the scale maps the largest weight in the group onto qmax: **s = max |w| / qmax**, the **absmax** rule;
- quantize: **q = round(w / s)**, kept between −qmax and qmax;
- dequantize: **ŵ = s · q**.

For the weights 0.5, −1.0, 0.25 in int4: s = 1.0 / 7 ≈ 0.1429, so q = round(3.5) = 4, round(−7) = −7 and round(1.75) = 2, and ŵ = 0.571, −1.0, 0.286. Rounding moves each weight by at most s/2, here about 0.071. (Python's `round` sends an exact half to the even neighbour, so 3.5 goes to 4 and 2.5 to 2; either way the error is at most s/2.)

```python
scale = 0.2 / 127                 # an int8 group whose largest weight is 0.2
q = round(0.0731 / scale)
print(q, q * scale)               # 46 0.07244094488188978
```

### Granularity: who shares a scale

One scale per group is the design choice that matters most.

- **Per-tensor**: one scale for a whole weight matrix.
- **Per-channel**: one scale per output row.
- **Per-group**: one scale for every g consecutive weights in a row, with g usually 32, 64 or 128.

The reason is **outliers**. Trained weights are mostly tiny, with a few much larger values. One outlier sets the scale for its whole group. Take the int4 group 0.01, −0.02, 0.03, 8.0: s = 8.0 / 7 ≈ 1.14, and every small weight rounds to 0. A small group confines the damage to its own few neighbours. On a test vector of 4,096 weights, mostly between −0.05 and 0.05 with four outliers near ±1.5, int4 gives:

| scales | weights per scale | mean squared error |
|---|---|---|
| per-tensor | 4,096 | 8.5 × 10⁻⁴ |
| one per 1,024 | 1,024 | 8.5 × 10⁻⁴ |
| one per 128 | 128 | 1.1 × 10⁻⁴ |
| one per 32 | 32 | 3.0 × 10⁻⁵ |

### The cost of the scales

Scales are stored too, usually in 16-bit. int4 with one 16-bit scale per 128 weights costs **4 + 16/128 = 4.125 bits per weight**. A 7B model then needs about 7 × 10⁹ × 4.125 / 8 ≈ 3.6 GB instead of 14 GB. Groups of 32 cost 4.5 bits per weight: better accuracy, slightly more bytes.

### Measuring the error

Never trust a quantization scheme you have not measured. Three numbers are standard, all over the original weights w and the dequantized ŵ:

- **mean squared error**: the average of (w − ŵ)²;
- **maximum absolute error**: the largest |w − ŵ|, at most s/2 for each group's s;
- **relative error**: √(Σ (w − ŵ)²) / √(Σ w²), the error's size compared with the weights' size.

Small weight errors are not the end of the story: what matters is the error in the layer's outputs and, in the end, in the model's answers. Real pipelines check a quantized model on held-out text and benchmarks, and the rule of thumb is that int8 is nearly free while int4 needs per-group scales or smarter rounding to stay close.

### Weight-only or activations too

- **[[Weight-only quantization|weight-only]]** (written W4A16: 4-bit weights, 16-bit activations) stores weights in int4 and turns them back into 16-bit inside the matrix-multiply kernel, just before use. The bytes read from memory shrink four-fold; the arithmetic stays 16-bit. It speeds up memory-bound decode, and does almost nothing for compute-bound prefill.
- **Weight-and-activation quantization** (W8A8) quantizes the activations too, so the multiply itself runs on 8-bit integer units, which on most accelerators do about twice the FLOPs of 16-bit ones. That speeds up compute-bound work, prefill and big batches. It is harder, because [[activations have outliers|activation-outliers]] of their own, which change with every input.
- The **KV cache** can be quantized the same way, often to [[8 bits|fp8]], halving the cache bytes that lesson 1 showed dominate long-context decode.

In a quantized matrix multiply the scale is applied once per group, not once per weight: a row's integer weights are multiplied by the input and summed, and the sum is multiplied by the scale. The practice builds that.

**Watch out:**

- **Scale from max |w|, not max w.** A group whose largest weight is negative would get a scale too small, and its most negative weights would be clipped.
- **A group of zeros has scale 0.** Dividing by it fails; store q = 0 for the whole group.
- **Clamp after rounding.** With other scale rules, rounding can land one step outside the range.
- **Count the scales in the bytes.** 4-bit weights with tiny groups can cost much more than 4 bits each.

::: context weight-only Why the arithmetic can stay 16-bit
In a memory-bound decode step the arithmetic units are mostly idle (lesson 1), so it does not matter that they work in 16-bit: what matters is the bytes crossing from memory. A weight-only kernel reads packed 4-bit integers and their scales, expands them to 16-bit values in fast on-chip memory, and multiplies as usual. Methods such as GPTQ and AWQ choose the integers more cleverly than plain rounding, by using a little calibration data to minimise the error in each layer's outputs, but they store weights in exactly this format.
:::

::: context activation-outliers The few loud channels
In large transformers a handful of hidden dimensions carry activations tens or hundreds of times larger than the rest, consistently across tokens. Quantizing activations per tensor would waste nearly all the integer range on those few channels. The fixes are to keep the loud channels in 16-bit and quantize the rest, to give each token its own scale, or to move part of the activations' range into the weights with a per-channel rescaling before quantizing both, which is the idea behind smoothing methods. All of them are ways of choosing who shares a scale, the theme of this lesson.
:::

::: context fp8 Integers or tiny floats
Recent accelerators also support 8-bit floating-point formats, which you simulated in ML Systems I. A float spends some bits on an exponent, so its marks are dense near zero and sparse far away, a good match for bell-shaped weights and activations; an integer's marks are evenly spaced. In practice fp8 with per-tensor or per-block scales has become a common choice for weights, activations and the KV cache in serving, while int4 with per-group scales remains the usual way to fit large models onto smaller accelerators.
:::
--- task
The starter has `make_weights(seed, n, outliers)`, which makes a test vector of `n` small weights with a few large outliers. Write symmetric absmax quantization by groups, and its measurement. `w` is a list of floats, `bits` is from 2 to 8, and `group` is a positive whole number or `None`.

- `quantize(w, bits, group=None)`: split `w` into groups of `group` consecutive values (the last group may be shorter; `None` means one group for the whole list). For each group, s = max |w| / qmax with qmax = 2^(bits−1) − 1, and each q = `round(w / s)` clamped to [−qmax, qmax]. A group whose values are all 0 has s = 0.0 and every q = 0. Return `(q, scales)`: the list of all integers, in order, and the list of scales, one per group.
- `dequantize(q, scales, group=None)`: the list of s · q, with each value using its own group's scale.
- `quant_error(w, bits, group=None)`: quantize, dequantize, and return a dict with `"mse"` (the mean of (w − ŵ)²), `"max_abs"` (the largest |w − ŵ|) and `"rel"` (√Σ(w − ŵ)² / √Σw²). Round each to 6 significant digits with `float(f"{x:.6g}")`. `w` is never all zeros here.

For example, `quantize([0.5, -1.0, 0.25, 0.0], 4, 2)` gives `q` `[4, -7, 7, 0]` and two scales, 1/7 and 0.25/7.
--- starter


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    return [round(x) for x in w], [1.0]


def dequantize(q, scales, group=None):
    return [float(v) for v in q]


def quant_error(w, bits, group=None):
    return {"mse": 0.0, "max_abs": 0.0, "rel": 0.0}
--- solution


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales


def dequantize(q, scales, group=None):
    size = group or len(q)
    return [v * scales[i // size] for i, v in enumerate(q)]


def sig(x):
    return float(f"{x:.6g}")


def quant_error(w, bits, group=None):
    back = dequantize(*quantize(w, bits, group), group)
    diffs = [a - b for a, b in zip(w, back)]
    mse = sum(d * d for d in diffs) / len(w)
    rel = math.sqrt(sum(d * d for d in diffs) / sum(x * x for x in w))
    return {"mse": sig(mse), "max_abs": sig(max(abs(d) for d in diffs)), "rel": sig(rel)}


--- hint
Let `size = group or len(w)` and loop `for start in range(0, len(w), size)`, taking `w[start:start + size]` as one group. Its scale is `max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax`.
--- hint
Quantize each value with `max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale)))`, and handle a scale of 0 before dividing. In `dequantize`, value i belongs to group `i // size`.
--- hint
For the error, dequantize what `quantize` returns, take the differences, and build the three numbers from them; `math.sqrt` gives the square roots.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda r: (r[0], [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]]))(quantize([0.5, -1.0, 0.25, 0.0], 4, 2))
=> ([4, -7, 7, 0], [0.142857143, 0.035714286])
--- check case | Per-tensor: one scale for everything
(lambda r: (r[0], [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]]))(quantize([0.5, -1.0, 0.25, 0.0], 4))
=> ([4, -7, 2, 0], [0.142857143])
--- check case | int8 has 127 steps each way, and the largest magnitude maps to ±127
quantize([0.1, -0.2, 0.05, 0.2], 8)[0]
=> [63, -127, 32, 127]
--- check case | A shorter last group, and a group of zeros
(lambda r: (r[0], [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]]))(quantize([0.0, 0.0, 0.0, 3.5, -0.7], 4, 3))
=> ([0, 0, 0, 7, -1], [0.0, 0.5])
--- check case | dequantize uses each value's own group scale
[round(x, 9) for x in dequantize([4, -7, 7, 0], [0.5, 0.25], 2)]
=> [2.0, -3.5, 1.75, 0.0]
--- check test | Every dequantized weight is within half a step of the original
(lambda w: (lambda q, s: all(abs(a - b) <= s[i // 64] / 2 + 1e-12 for i, (a, b) in enumerate(zip(w, dequantize(q, s, 64)))))(*quantize(w, 4, 64)))(make_weights(3, 1000, 3))
--- check case | quant_error on the lesson's test vector, int4 per-tensor and per-128
(quant_error(make_weights(1, 4096, 4), 4), quant_error(make_weights(1, 4096, 4), 4, 128))
=> ({'mse': 0.000853153, 'max_abs': 0.0710198, 'rel': 0.56551}, {'mse': 0.000108022, 'max_abs': 0.0499269, 'rel': 0.201225})
--- check test | Smaller groups and more bits both cut the error
(lambda w: quant_error(w, 4, 32)["mse"] < quant_error(w, 4, 128)["mse"] < quant_error(w, 4)["mse"] and quant_error(w, 8, 128)["mse"] < quant_error(w, 4, 128)["mse"] / 50)(make_weights(2, 4096, 6))
--- check case | A vector without outliers is easy even per-tensor
quant_error(make_weights(5, 512), 8)
=> {'mse': 1.30328e-08, 'max_abs': 0.000196359, 'rel': 0.00389126}

+++ question | One outlier
--- ask
In int4 with one scale per tensor, a weight matrix whose values are mostly within ±0.05 but has a single weight of 6.0 comes out badly damaged. What exactly goes wrong?
--- choice
The 6.0 is clipped to 0.05, which changes the layer's output.
--- choice correct
The scale becomes 6.0 / 7 ≈ 0.86, so every small weight rounds to 0.
--- choice
int4 cannot store negative numbers, so half the weights are lost.
--- choice
The outlier overflows the 4-bit integer and wraps around to a negative value.
--- why
Absmax puts the largest magnitude at qmax, so the outlier is stored almost exactly, and the step between integers becomes 0.86. A weight of 0.05 is about 0.06 steps: it rounds to 0. Smaller groups give the small weights their own, finer scale.

+++ question | Bits per weight
--- ask
Weights are stored as int4 with one 16-bit scale for every 64 weights. How many bits does each weight cost, scales included? Type a decimal number.
--- answer
4.25
4.25 bits
--- why
4 bits for the integer, plus 16 bits of scale shared by 64 weights: 16 / 64 = 0.25 bits each. 4.25 in all.

+++ question | Faster decode, same prefill
--- ask
Switching a 7B model from 16-bit weights to int4 weight-only quantization makes decode at batch 1 about 3.8 times faster, but prefill of a long prompt barely changes. Why?
--- choice
Prefill reads the weights once per token, so it saves nothing.
--- choice
The int4 weights must be converted back to 16-bit before prefill, which takes as long as reading them.
--- choice correct
Decode is memory-bound, so a quarter of the bytes means about a quarter of the time; prefill is compute-bound, and weight-only quantization leaves the 16-bit arithmetic unchanged.
--- choice
Prefill uses the KV cache, which is still 16-bit.
--- why
A step's time is the larger of its compute time and its memory time. Weight-only quantization shrinks only the memory side. Decode at batch 1 is almost all memory time; prefill of a long prompt is almost all compute time. Speeding up prefill needs activations quantized too, so the multiply itself runs on faster integer units.

+++ practice | How big is the model?
--- task
Write `model_bytes(n_params, bits, group=None, scale_bits=16)`: the bytes needed to store `n_params` weights quantized to `bits` bits each, with one scale of `scale_bits` bits for every `group` weights (⌈n_params / group⌉ scales; `group=None` means a single scale for everything). Round the total number of bits **up** to whole bytes. Then write `bits_per_weight(bits, group, scale_bits=16)`: the bits each weight costs on average for a very large tensor, bits + scale_bits / group, as a float (just `float(bits)` when `group` is `None`).

For example, `model_bytes(7_000_000_000, 4, 128)` is 3,609,375,000 and `bits_per_weight(4, 128)` is 4.125.
--- starter
def model_bytes(n_params, bits, group=None, scale_bits=16):
    return n_params * bits // 8


def bits_per_weight(bits, group, scale_bits=16):
    return float(bits)
--- solution
def model_bytes(n_params, bits, group=None, scale_bits=16):
    scales = 1 if group is None else -(-n_params // group)
    total_bits = n_params * bits + scales * scale_bits
    return -(-total_bits // 8)


def bits_per_weight(bits, group, scale_bits=16):
    if group is None:
        return float(bits)
    return bits + scale_bits / group
--- hint
The number of scales is the number of groups, rounded up: `-(-n_params // group)`. Count everything in bits first.
--- hint
Round bits up to bytes the same way: `-(-total_bits // 8)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(model_bytes(7_000_000_000, 4, 128), bits_per_weight(4, 128))
=> (3609375000, 4.125)
--- check case | A 70B model in int8 per-channel-like groups of 8,192, and in int4 groups of 32
(model_bytes(70_000_000_000, 8, 8192), model_bytes(70_000_000_000, 4, 32))
=> (70017089844, 39375000000)
--- check case | One scale for everything
model_bytes(1000, 4)
=> 502
--- check case | Rounding up: groups and bytes
(model_bytes(100, 3, 64), model_bytes(1, 4, 128))
=> (42, 3)
--- check case | Bits per weight with small groups and with 8-bit scales
(bits_per_weight(4, 32), bits_per_weight(4, 32, 8), bits_per_weight(8, None))
=> (4.5, 4.25, 8.0)

+++ practice | A matrix-vector product on integer weights
--- task
Real kernels never rebuild the float weights. Write two functions for a weight matrix `W` given as a list of rows (one row per output):

- `quantize_rows(W, bits)`: per-channel symmetric absmax quantization, one scale per row (a row of zeros has scale 0.0 and all-zero integers). Return `(Q, scales)`, where `Q` is the list of integer rows.
- `int_matvec(Q, scales, x)`: the product with a float vector `x`, done the way a kernel does it: for each row i, first the sum of `Q[i][j] * x[j]` over j, then **one** multiplication by `scales[i]`. Return the list of outputs.

The checks compare `int_matvec` with the exact float product.
--- starter


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize_rows(W, bits):
    return [[round(v) for v in row] for row in W], [1.0] * len(W)


def int_matvec(Q, scales, x):
    return [sum(q * v for q, v in zip(row, x)) for row in Q]
--- solution


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize_rows(W, bits):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    Q, scales = [], []
    for row in W:
        s = max(abs(v) for v in row) / qmax
        scales.append(s)
        if s == 0:
            Q.append([0] * len(row))
        else:
            Q.append([max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(v / s))) for v in row])
    return Q, scales


def int_matvec(Q, scales, x):
    return [s * sum(q * v for q, v in zip(row, x)) for row, s in zip(Q, scales)]
--- hint
`quantize_rows` is the lesson's quantization applied to each row on its own, with that row's own absmax scale.
--- hint
Because every weight in row i shares the scale s_i, Σ_j (s_i · q_ij) · x_j = s_i · Σ_j q_ij · x_j: sum first, then scale once.
--- check case | A 2 × 3 matrix in int4
(lambda r: (r[0], [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]]))(quantize_rows([[0.7, -0.35, 0.1], [0.0, 0.0, 0.0]], 4))
=> ([[7, -4, 1], [0, 0, 0]], [0.1, 0.0])
--- check case | The integer product, scaled once per row
[round(v, 9) for v in int_matvec([[7, -4, 1], [2, 0, -1]], [0.1, 0.5], [1.0, 2.0, 3.0])]
=> [0.2, -0.5]
--- check test | int8 per row stays close to the exact product
(lambda W, x: (lambda y, Q, s: all(abs(a - b) < 0.01 for a, b in zip(y, int_matvec(Q, s, x))))([sum(w * v for w, v in zip(row, x)) for row in W], *quantize_rows(W, 8)))([make_weights(i, 64, 1) for i in range(16)], make_weights(99, 64))
--- check test | int4 per row is much rougher than int8 on rows with outliers
(lambda W, x: (lambda y: (lambda e4, e8: e4 > 5 * e8)(max(abs(a - b) for a, b in zip(y, int_matvec(*quantize_rows(W, 4), x))), max(abs(a - b) for a, b in zip(y, int_matvec(*quantize_rows(W, 8), x)))))([sum(w * v for w, v in zip(row, x)) for row in W]))([make_weights(i, 64, 1) for i in range(16)], make_weights(99, 64))
--- check case | Rows with very different sizes each get their own scale
(lambda r: [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]])(quantize_rows([[1.27, 0.0], [0.0127, -0.00635]], 8))
=> [0.01, 0.0001]

+++ practice | What int4 buys in the cost model
--- task
The starter has lesson 1's cost model. Weight-only quantization changes only the bytes of the weights: a model stored with `bits` per weight and one 16-bit scale per `group` weights has `bytes_per_param = (bits + 16 / group) / 8`, while its arithmetic stays the same.

Write `int4_gains(model, hw, ctx, batch, prompt_len, bits, group)`. Build a copy of `model` with that `bytes_per_param` (keep every other field, and do not change `model`). Return a tuple `(decode_speedup, prefill_speedup, freed_gb)`:

- `decode_speedup`: the time of a decode step of `batch` requests with `ctx` cached tokens each (`[(1, ctx)] * batch`) on the original model divided by its time on the quantized one;
- `prefill_speedup`: the same for one prefill of `prompt_len` tokens (`[(prompt_len, 0)]`);
- `freed_gb`: the weight memory saved, (old weight bytes − new weight bytes) / 10⁹.

Round each to 3 decimal places.
--- starter


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def int4_gains(model, hw, ctx, batch, prompt_len, bits, group):
    return (1.0, 1.0, 0.0)
--- solution


import math
import random


class Model:
    """The shapes of a decoder-only transformer that set what serving it costs."""

    def __init__(self, n_params, n_layers, d_model, n_kv_heads, head_dim, bytes_per_param=2, kv_bytes=2):
        self.n_params = n_params
        self.n_layers = n_layers
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.n_kv_heads = n_kv_heads
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.bytes_per_param = bytes_per_param
        self.kv_bytes = kv_bytes


class Accel:
    """One accelerator: peak FLOP/s, memory bandwidth in bytes/s, memory size in bytes."""

    def __init__(self, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


def kv_bytes_per_token(model):
    return 2 * model.n_layers * model.n_kv_heads * model.head_dim * model.kv_bytes


def step_cost(model, hw, work):
    if not work:
        return {"flops": 0, "bytes": 0, "time": 0.0, "bound": "idle"}
    tokens = sum(n for n, _ in work)
    keys = sum(n * c + n * (n + 1) // 2 for n, c in work)
    flops = 2 * model.n_params * tokens + 4 * model.n_layers * model.d_model * keys
    kv = kv_bytes_per_token(model)
    moved = model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param + kv * sum(c + n for n, c in work)
    t_compute = flops / hw.peak_flops
    t_memory = moved / hw.mem_bw
    bound = "compute" if t_compute > t_memory else "memory"
    return {"flops": flops, "bytes": moved, "time": max(t_compute, t_memory), "bound": bound}


def int4_gains(model, hw, ctx, batch, prompt_len, bits, group):
    small = Model(model.n_params, model.n_layers, model.d_model, model.n_kv_heads, model.head_dim,
                  (bits + 16 / group) / 8, model.kv_bytes)

    def ratio(work):
        return step_cost(model, hw, work)["time"] / step_cost(small, hw, work)["time"]

    freed = (model.n_params * model.bytes_per_param - small.n_params * small.bytes_per_param) / 1e9
    return (round(ratio([(1, ctx)] * batch), 3), round(ratio([(prompt_len, 0)]), 3), round(freed, 3))
--- hint
Make the quantized model with `Model(...)`, passing every field of `model` except `bytes_per_param`, which becomes `(bits + 16 / group) / 8`.
--- hint
Each speedup is old time over new time for the same work list. Decode at small batch is memory-bound, so it speeds up a lot; a long prefill is compute-bound, so it barely moves.
--- check case | The 7B model on the lesson-1 accelerator, int4 with groups of 128
int4_gains(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, 1, 2000, 4, 128)
=> (3.778, 1.0, 10.391)
--- check case | At batch 64 the KV cache reads limit the gain
int4_gains(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, 64, 2000, 4, 128)
=> (1.865, 1.0, 10.391)
--- check case | int8 with groups of 64
int4_gains(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128), Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 500, 1, 100, 8, 64)
=> (1.931, 1.938, 6.781)
--- check test | The model passed in is not changed
(lambda m: (int4_gains(m, Accel(1e15, 3.35e12, 80e9), 1000, 1, 2000, 4, 128), m.bytes_per_param)[1] == 2)(Model(7_000_000_000, 32, 4096, 8, 128))

+++ practice | Asymmetric quantization for values that are never negative
--- task
Activations after a ReLU are never negative, so symmetric quantization wastes half its integers on values that never occur. **Asymmetric** quantization maps the range [min, max] onto the integers 0 to 2^bits − 1 with a scale and a **zero point**:

- s = (max − min) / (2^bits − 1);
- z = round(−min / s), the integer that stands for the real value 0;
- q = round(x / s) + z, clamped to [0, 2^bits − 1]; and x̂ = s · (q − z).

Write `quantize_asym(x, bits)` returning `(q, s, z)`, and `asym_error(x, bits)` returning the mean squared error of x̂ against `x`, rounded to 6 significant digits with `float(f"{v:.6g}")`. If every value is the same (max equals min), use s = 0.0, z = 0 and every q = 0, and dequantize as the value itself: x̂ = min. (Handle that case in `asym_error` too, so its error is 0.0.)

For example, `quantize_asym([0.0, 1.0, 0.5], 2)` is `([0, 3, 2], 0.3333333333333333, 0)`.
--- starter


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales


def dequantize(q, scales, group=None):
    size = group or len(q)
    return [v * scales[i // size] for i, v in enumerate(q)]


def sig(x):
    return float(f"{x:.6g}")


def quant_error(w, bits, group=None):
    back = dequantize(*quantize(w, bits, group), group)
    diffs = [a - b for a, b in zip(w, back)]
    mse = sum(d * d for d in diffs) / len(w)
    rel = math.sqrt(sum(d * d for d in diffs) / sum(x * x for x in w))
    return {"mse": sig(mse), "max_abs": sig(max(abs(d) for d in diffs)), "rel": sig(rel)}


def quantize_asym(x, bits):
    return quantize(x, bits)[0], 1.0, 0


def asym_error(x, bits):
    return quant_error(x, bits)["mse"]
--- solution


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales


def dequantize(q, scales, group=None):
    size = group or len(q)
    return [v * scales[i // size] for i, v in enumerate(q)]


def sig(x):
    return float(f"{x:.6g}")


def quant_error(w, bits, group=None):
    back = dequantize(*quantize(w, bits, group), group)
    diffs = [a - b for a, b in zip(w, back)]
    mse = sum(d * d for d in diffs) / len(w)
    rel = math.sqrt(sum(d * d for d in diffs) / sum(x * x for x in w))
    return {"mse": sig(mse), "max_abs": sig(max(abs(d) for d in diffs)), "rel": sig(rel)}


def quantize_asym(x, bits):
    lo, hi = min(x), max(x)
    top = 2 ** bits - 1
    if hi == lo:
        return [0] * len(x), 0.0, 0
    s = (hi - lo) / top
    z = round(-lo / s)
    return [max(0, min(top, round(v / s) + z)) for v in x], s, z


def asym_error(x, bits):
    q, s, z = quantize_asym(x, bits)
    if s == 0:
        back = [min(x)] * len(x)
    else:
        back = [s * (v - z) for v in q]
    return float(f"{sum((a - b) ** 2 for a, b in zip(x, back)) / len(x):.6g}")
--- hint
Compute the scale from the range, max − min, spread over 2^bits − 1 steps. The zero point is the integer that −min / s rounds to.
--- hint
Handle max == min before dividing. Dequantize with `s * (q - z)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
quantize_asym([0.0, 1.0, 0.5], 2)
=> ([0, 3, 2], 0.3333333333333333, 0)
--- check case | A range that does not start at 0 has a zero point
(lambda r: (r[0], round(r[1], 9), r[2]))(quantize_asym([-1.0, 0.0, 2.0, 0.5], 4))
=> ([0, 5, 15, 7], 0.2, 5)
--- check case | All values equal
(quantize_asym([0.3, 0.3, 0.3], 8), asym_error([0.3, 0.3, 0.3], 8))
=> (([0, 0, 0], 0.0, 0), 0.0)
--- check test | On non-negative data, asymmetric int4 beats symmetric int4
(lambda x: asym_error(x, 4) < quant_error(x, 4)["mse"] / 2)([abs(v) for v in make_weights(8, 2000)])
--- check case | The error, measured
(asym_error([abs(v) for v in make_weights(8, 2000)], 4), quant_error([abs(v) for v in make_weights(8, 2000)], 4)["mse"])
=> (9.21573e-07, 4.08877e-06)

+++ practice | Debug: clipped negative weights
--- task
This `quantize` should implement symmetric absmax quantization by groups, exactly as in the lesson. On most groups it works. But on a group whose largest magnitude belongs to a **negative** weight, such as `[0.1, -0.7, 0.2]` in int4, it returns `[1, -7, 3]` with a scale of 0.2/7, so −0.7 comes back as −0.2: clipped. The right answer is `[1, -7, 2]` with a scale of 0.1. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales
--- solution
def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales
--- hint
Which value does `max(chunk)` pick in `[0.1, -0.7, 0.2]`? Which value should set the scale?
--- hint
The scale must come from the largest **magnitude**: `max(abs(x) for x in chunk)`. With the plain maximum, a group of all-negative weights even gets a negative scale.
--- check case | The group from the task
(lambda r: (r[0], [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]]))(quantize([0.1, -0.7, 0.2], 4))
=> ([1, -7, 2], [0.1])
--- check case | A group of negative weights
(lambda r: (r[0], [round(s, 9) for s in r[1]]))(quantize([-0.5, -1.0, -0.25, -0.75], 8, 2))
=> ([-64, -127, -42, -127], [0.007874016, 0.005905512])
--- check case | Groups with positive maxima are unchanged
quantize([0.1, -0.2, 0.05, 0.2], 8)[0]
=> [63, -127, 32, 127]
--- check case | Zeros still give scale 0
quantize([0.0, 0.0], 4)
=> ([0, 0], [0.0])

+++ practice | Choosing a group size under a memory budget
--- task
Smaller groups are more accurate but store more scales. Write `best_group(w, bits, candidates, budget)`. For each group size g in `candidates` whose storage cost, bits + 16/g bits per weight, is at most `budget`, measure the mean squared error of quantizing `w` with that group size (symmetric absmax, as in the lesson; the starter has `quantize`, `dequantize` and `quant_error`). Return the pair `(g, mse)` for the candidate with the smallest error (on a tie, the larger g, which costs less), with the error as `quant_error` reports it. Return `None` if no candidate fits the budget.
--- starter


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales


def dequantize(q, scales, group=None):
    size = group or len(q)
    return [v * scales[i // size] for i, v in enumerate(q)]


def sig(x):
    return float(f"{x:.6g}")


def quant_error(w, bits, group=None):
    back = dequantize(*quantize(w, bits, group), group)
    diffs = [a - b for a, b in zip(w, back)]
    mse = sum(d * d for d in diffs) / len(w)
    rel = math.sqrt(sum(d * d for d in diffs) / sum(x * x for x in w))
    return {"mse": sig(mse), "max_abs": sig(max(abs(d) for d in diffs)), "rel": sig(rel)}


def best_group(w, bits, candidates, budget):
    return None
--- solution


import math
import random


def make_weights(seed, n, outliers=0):
    """n small weights, uniform in [-0.05, 0.05), with a few large outliers at random places."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    w = [(rng.random() - 0.5) * 0.1 for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(outliers):
        w[rng.randrange(n)] = (rng.random() + 1.0) * (1 if rng.random() < 0.5 else -1)
    return w


def quantize(w, bits, group=None):
    qmax = 2 ** (bits - 1) - 1
    size = group or len(w)
    q, scales = [], []
    for start in range(0, len(w), size):
        chunk = w[start:start + size]
        scale = max(abs(x) for x in chunk) / qmax
        scales.append(scale)
        if scale == 0:
            q += [0] * len(chunk)
        else:
            q += [max(-qmax, min(qmax, round(x / scale))) for x in chunk]
    return q, scales


def dequantize(q, scales, group=None):
    size = group or len(q)
    return [v * scales[i // size] for i, v in enumerate(q)]


def sig(x):
    return float(f"{x:.6g}")


def quant_error(w, bits, group=None):
    back = dequantize(*quantize(w, bits, group), group)
    diffs = [a - b for a, b in zip(w, back)]
    mse = sum(d * d for d in diffs) / len(w)
    rel = math.sqrt(sum(d * d for d in diffs) / sum(x * x for x in w))
    return {"mse": sig(mse), "max_abs": sig(max(abs(d) for d in diffs)), "rel": sig(rel)}


def best_group(w, bits, candidates, budget):
    best = None
    for g in candidates:
        if bits + 16 / g > budget:
            continue
        mse = quant_error(w, bits, g)["mse"]
        if best is None or mse < best[1] or (mse == best[1] and g > best[0]):
            best = (g, mse)
    return best
--- hint
Skip every candidate whose bits + 16/g is over the budget, then call `quant_error(w, bits, g)` for the rest.
--- hint
Keep the best pair seen so far, replacing it when the error is smaller, or equal with a larger g.
--- check case | Within 4.25 bits per weight, groups of 64 are the finest allowed
best_group(make_weights(1, 4096, 4), 4, [32, 64, 128, 256], 4.25)
=> (64, 5.59917e-05)
--- check case | A looser budget allows groups of 32
best_group(make_weights(1, 4096, 4), 4, [256, 128, 64, 32], 4.5)
=> (32, 3.0459e-05)
--- check case | Nothing fits
best_group(make_weights(1, 4096, 4), 4, [16, 32], 4.25)
=> None
--- check case | A tie goes to the larger group
best_group([0.5] * 256, 4, [64, 128], 5.0)
=> (128, 0.0)

=== mlsys2-06 | Prefix caching and KV-aware routing
--- teach
Lesson 2 let two sequences share KV blocks when one was forked from the other. In real traffic the sharing is far wider: thousands of requests begin with the same system prompt, every turn of a chat repeats the whole conversation so far, and agents resend the same tool descriptions on every call. If the server keeps the KV cache of a prefix after its request ends, the next request with that prefix skips its prefill. This lesson builds that cache as a radix tree, and then the router that sends each request to the replica most likely to have its prefix.

### What a prefix cache saves

A prompt's KV entries depend only on the tokens up to that point: the keys and values at position 100 are fixed by tokens 1 to 100, whatever comes after (that is causality again). So if a request's first 800 tokens match a cached prefix, the server only prefills the last 200, attending to the 800 cached ones. In the lesson-1 cost model that is the work item `(200, 800)` instead of `(1000, 0)`: about a fifth of the FLOPs, so a much shorter time to first token, and less work taken from everyone else's steps.

One edge: if the whole prompt is cached, the server still runs the **last** token through the model, because it needs that position's logits to sample the first output token.

### A tree of prefixes

The cache must answer one question fast: **what is the longest cached prefix of this prompt?** A **trie** answers it: a tree where each edge is labelled with a token, so each path from the root spells a token sequence, and matching a prompt means walking down as far as its tokens allow. Prompts are thousands of tokens long, though, and a trie needs a node per token.

A **[[radix tree|radix]]** compresses the trie: an edge carries a whole run of tokens, so a long stretch with no branching is one node. Picture three prompts, as token runs:

```text
root
 └─ [sys A: 200 tokens]
     ├─ [user 1: 40 tokens]
     └─ [user 2: 25 tokens]
```

Matching walks from the root. At each node, the child is picked by the next token (a dict from first token to child), then the child's run is compared token by token with the prompt. A full match moves down; a partial match ends the walk inside that run.

Inserting a prompt walks the same way. Where the prompt leaves the tree:

- at a node, with no child for the next token: hang a new leaf there holding the rest of the prompt;
- in the **middle** of a run: **split** that node in two, the shared part and the remainder, and hang the new leaf off the shared part.

For example, a tree holding `[5, 6, 7, 8]` gets `[5, 6, 9]` inserted: the node `[5, 6, 7, 8]` splits into `[5, 6]` with child `[7, 8]`, and a new leaf `[9]` joins under `[5, 6]`. The tree now stores 5 tokens, not 7: the shared `5, 6` is stored once.

```python
a, b = (5, 6, 7, 8), (5, 6, 9)
n = 0
while n < min(len(a), len(b)) and a[n] == b[n]:
    n += 1
print(n, a[:n], a[n:], b[n:])     # 2 (5, 6) (7, 8) (9,)
```

### Eviction

The cache lives in the same accelerator memory as the running requests' caches, so it has a budget, and when it is full something must go. The standard policy is **least recently used** (LRU): every node remembers the last time a request used it, and the cache evicts the node used longest ago.

Only **leaves** may be evicted. An inner node's tokens are the beginning of every prefix below it: removing `[sys A]` would make `[user 1]` meaningless. Removing a leaf removes only its own run, and its parent may become a leaf that is evictable later. Since a request that uses a leaf also passes through all its ancestors, a parent is always used at least as recently as its children, so LRU on leaves takes the coldest tails first and keeps hot shared prefixes, such as a system prompt, to the end. (A real cache also **pins** the nodes of requests that are running right now, so they cannot be evicted mid-flight; the lab leaves that out.)

### Many replicas: where to send a request

A large service runs many **replicas** of the model, each a server with its own memory and its own prefix cache. A **router** in front decides which replica gets each request. That choice now matters twice:

- **Load.** Sending a request to a busy replica makes it queue behind the work already there: **[[head-of-line blocking|hol]]**, the same waiting-behind-slower-work that long prefills caused inside one server in lesson 3, now between machines. Plain load balancing picks the **least-loaded** replica, or **round-robin** (each replica in turn).
- **Cache hits.** A replica that has the request's prefix cached skips most of its prefill. Least-loaded routing scatters requests that share a system prompt across all replicas, so every replica must cache every prefix, and each gets fewer hits.

A **KV-aware router** (also called cache-aware or prefix-aware) estimates, for each replica, how long a prefix it has cached, usually from a copy of each replica's radix tree kept at the router, and scores each replica on both: for example, **score = matched tokens − α × load**, with α (alpha) a weight you choose, picking the highest. With α = 0 it chases hits and can pile all traffic for a popular prefix onto one replica; with a large α it becomes least-loaded. Production routers add a guard: prefer the cache match unless that replica's load is too far above the least loaded, and then balance instead. This lesson's practice measures all of these.

**[[Session affinity|affinity]]** is the simplest prefix-aware policy: send every turn of one conversation to the same replica, by hashing the session id. Each turn repeats the conversation so far, so the replica that served the last turn has almost the whole prompt cached.

**Watch out:**

- **Evict leaves only.** Removing an inner node strands its children's tokens.
- **Split on partial matches.** Inserting without splitting either duplicates the shared tokens or loses the old suffix.
- **Matching for a score must not refresh LRU times.** Looking at a replica's cache to decide where to route is not a use; only the replica that serves the request should touch its nodes.
- **A fully cached prompt still needs one token of prefill** to produce the first output token's logits.

::: context radix A tree with long edges
A radix tree, also called a compressed trie or Patricia tree, stores strings so that any two share their common beginning once. Routers in computer networks use the same structure to find the longest matching address prefix for every packet. For prefix caching, one serving engine popularised a radix tree over tokens in 2024, and other engines keep the same information as a hash per KV block, where a block's hash covers all tokens up to its end: two requests share a block exactly when their prompts agree up to that block.
:::

::: context hol One slow request in front
Head-of-line blocking is the delay a request suffers because the one in front of it is slow, like a queue at a counter behind someone with a complicated order. In serving it appears inside a replica, when a huge prefill stalls a step, and across replicas, when the router sends work to a replica that is already busy with long prompts while another sits idle. Balancing on queue length alone is not enough, because one queued request with a 100,000-token prompt is worth hundreds of short ones; routers therefore estimate load in tokens of pending prefill, not in requests.
:::

::: context affinity Sticky sessions and consistent hashing
Session affinity sends every request with the same key to the same backend. Hashing the key modulo the number of replicas works until a replica is added or removed: then almost every key moves, and every cache goes cold at once. Consistent hashing, from Distributed Systems I, places replicas at many points on a hash ring and sends a key to the next point clockwise, so removing a replica moves only the keys that were on it. The same trick spreads cache keys over cache servers in web caching.
:::
--- task
Write a class `RadixCache(capacity)` that stores token prefixes in a radix tree, holding at most `capacity` tokens. Tokens are whole numbers; sequences are lists or tuples. Each node holds a run of tokens (a tuple), its children (a dict from a child's first token to the child), and `last_used`, the time a request last used it.

- `match(tokens, now=None)`: the length of the longest prefix of `tokens` stored in the tree. If `now` is given, set `last_used = now` on every node the walk enters, including a node where the match ends partway through its run. If `now` is `None`, change nothing.
- `insert(tokens, now=0)`: store `tokens`. Walk as in `match`, setting `last_used = now` on every node passed through or created. Where the sequence leaves the tree at a node, add a leaf with the rest; where it leaves partway through a node's run, split that node into the shared part (a new node, which also gets `last_used = now`) and the remainder (keeping its own children and `last_used`), and continue from the shared part. Then **evict**: while the tree holds more than `capacity` tokens, remove the leaf with the smallest `last_used`; on a tie, the leaf whose full sequence from the root is smallest (compare the tuples).
- `size()`: the number of tokens stored, the total length of all runs.

You do not need to merge a node with its only child after an eviction.

For example, after `insert([1, 2, 3, 4])` and `insert([1, 2, 5])`, `size()` is 5 and `match([1, 2, 5, 6])` is 3.
--- starter
class RadixCache:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.prompts = []

    def match(self, tokens, now=None):
        return 0

    def insert(self, tokens, now=0):
        self.prompts.append(list(tokens))

    def size(self):
        return sum(len(p) for p in self.prompts)

import random


def make_prompts(seed, n, n_system=4, system_len=200, user_len=(20, 80), vocab=1000, weights=None):
    """n chat prompts: one of n_system shared system prompts (chosen with the given weights,
    or uniformly), then the user's own tokens."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    systems = [[rng.randrange(vocab) for _ in range(system_len)] for _ in range(n_system)]
    out = []
    for _ in range(n):
        user = [rng.randrange(vocab) for _ in range(rng.randint(*user_len))]
        pick = rng.randrange(n_system) if weights is None else rng.choices(range(n_system), weights)[0]
        out.append(systems[pick] + user)
    return out


--- solution


class Node:
    def __init__(self, tokens, parent):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.parent = parent
        self.children = {}
        self.last_used = 0


def shared(a, b):
    n = 0
    while n < len(a) and n < len(b) and a[n] == b[n]:
        n += 1
    return n


class RadixCache:
    """Cached token prefixes in a radix tree, at most `capacity` tokens, evicting least recently used leaves."""

    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.root = Node((), None)
        self.total = 0

    def size(self):
        return self.total

    def match(self, tokens, now=None):
        tokens = tuple(tokens)
        node, i = self.root, 0
        while i < len(tokens) and tokens[i] in node.children:
            child = node.children[tokens[i]]
            n = shared(child.tokens, tokens[i:])
            if now is not None:
                child.last_used = now
            i += n
            if n < len(child.tokens):
                break
            node = child
        return i

    def insert(self, tokens, now=0):
        tokens = tuple(tokens)
        node, i = self.root, 0
        while i < len(tokens):
            child = node.children.get(tokens[i])
            if child is None:
                leaf = Node(tokens[i:], node)
                leaf.last_used = now
                node.children[tokens[i]] = leaf
                self.total += len(tokens) - i
                break
            n = shared(child.tokens, tokens[i:])
            if n < len(child.tokens):
                mid = Node(child.tokens[:n], node)
                node.children[tokens[i]] = mid
                child.tokens = child.tokens[n:]
                child.parent = mid
                mid.children[child.tokens[0]] = child
                child = mid
            child.last_used = now
            node = child
            i += n
        self.evict()

    def path(self, node):
        parts = []
        while node is not self.root:
            parts.append(node.tokens)
            node = node.parent
        return tuple(t for part in reversed(parts) for t in part)

    def evict(self):
        while self.total > self.capacity:
            leaves, stack = [], [self.root]
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                stack.extend(node.children.values())
                if not node.children and node is not self.root:
                    leaves.append(node)
            victim = min(leaves, key=lambda n: (n.last_used, self.path(n)))
            del victim.parent.children[victim.tokens[0]]
            self.total -= len(victim.tokens)


import random


def make_prompts(seed, n, n_system=4, system_len=200, user_len=(20, 80), vocab=1000, weights=None):
    """n chat prompts: one of n_system shared system prompts (chosen with the given weights,
    or uniformly), then the user's own tokens."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    systems = [[rng.randrange(vocab) for _ in range(system_len)] for _ in range(n_system)]
    out = []
    for _ in range(n):
        user = [rng.randrange(vocab) for _ in range(rng.randint(*user_len))]
        pick = rng.randrange(n_system) if weights is None else rng.choices(range(n_system), weights)[0]
        out.append(systems[pick] + user)
    return out


--- hint
Write a helper that counts how many leading tokens two sequences share. `match` walks from the root: pick the child by the next token, compare its run, add the shared count, and stop if the run was not matched in full.
--- hint
In `insert`, when the shared count is shorter than the child's run, split: make a node with `child.tokens[:n]` in the child's place, cut the child's run to `child.tokens[n:]`, and put the child under the new node. When no child starts with the next token, add a leaf with all remaining tokens and add their count to the size.
--- hint
For eviction, collect every leaf (a node with no children, other than the root), take the one with the smallest `(last_used, full sequence)`, delete it from its parent's children and subtract its run's length. Each node needs a link to its parent to rebuild its full sequence and to delete it.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda c: (c.insert([1, 2, 3, 4]), c.insert([1, 2, 5]), c.size(), c.match([1, 2, 5, 6]))[2:])(RadixCache(100))
=> (5, 3)
--- check case | Matching an empty cache, an empty prompt, and a prompt that ends early
(lambda c: (c.match([1, 2]), c.insert([7, 8, 9]), c.match([]), c.match([7, 8]), c.match([7, 8, 9, 10]), c.match([8])))(RadixCache(100))
=> (0, None, 0, 2, 3, 0)
--- check case | A split in the middle of a run stores the shared tokens once
(lambda c: (c.insert([5, 6, 7, 8]), c.insert([5, 6, 9]), c.insert([5, 6, 7, 1]), c.size(), c.match([5, 6, 7, 8]), c.match([5, 6, 7, 1]), c.match([5, 6, 2]))[3:])(RadixCache(100))
=> (6, 4, 4, 2)
--- check case | Inserting a prefix of a stored sequence adds nothing
(lambda c: (c.insert([1, 2, 3, 4, 5]), c.insert([1, 2, 3]), c.size(), c.match([1, 2, 3, 9]))[2:])(RadixCache(100))
=> (5, 3)
--- check case | Over capacity, the least recently used leaf goes first
(lambda c: (c.insert([1, 2, 3, 4], now=1), c.insert([1, 2, 7, 8], now=2), c.match([1, 2, 3, 4], now=3), c.insert([9, 9, 9], now=4), c.size(), c.match([1, 2, 3, 4]), c.match([1, 2, 7, 8]), c.match([9, 9, 9]))[4:])(RadixCache(8))
=> (7, 4, 2, 3)
?? The tree held 6 tokens; adding 3 makes 9. The leaf [7, 8] was last used at time 2, older than [3, 4] (touched by the match at time 3).
--- check case | Matching without a time does not protect a leaf
(lambda c: (c.insert([1, 2, 3, 4], now=1), c.insert([1, 2, 7, 8], now=2), c.match([1, 2, 3, 4]), c.insert([9, 9, 9], now=4), c.match([1, 2, 3, 4]), c.match([1, 2, 7, 8]))[4:])(RadixCache(8))
=> (2, 4)
--- check case | Evicting leaves can expose their parent, which may go next
(lambda c: (c.insert([1, 2, 3], now=1), c.insert([1, 2, 4], now=1), c.insert([5, 6, 7, 8, 9], now=2), c.size(), c.match([1, 2, 3]), c.match([1, 2, 4]), c.match([5, 6, 7, 8, 9]))[3:])(RadixCache(5))
=> (5, 0, 0, 5)
--- check case | A tie in time goes to the smallest sequence
(lambda c: (c.insert([4, 1], now=1), c.insert([2, 9], now=1), c.insert([3, 3], now=2), c.match([4, 1]), c.match([2, 9]), c.match([3, 3]))[3:])(RadixCache(4))
=> (2, 0, 2)
--- check case | Capacity 0 keeps nothing
(lambda c: (c.insert([1, 2, 3]), c.size(), c.match([1, 2, 3]))[1:])(RadixCache(0))
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | 300 chat prompts through a cache of 3,000 tokens: the hits and the size
(lambda c, ps: (sum((c.match(p, t), c.insert(p, t))[0] for t, p in enumerate(ps)), c.size()))(RadixCache(3000), make_prompts(1, 300))
=> (59202, 2980)
?? Each prompt is matched first (that is the request's hit), then inserted, at time t = its position in the list.

+++ question | Only leaves
--- ask
Why does a prefix cache evict only leaves of its radix tree, never an inner node?
--- choice
Because inner nodes are always smaller than leaves.
--- choice correct
Because an inner node's tokens begin every prefix below it; removing it would leave its descendants' tokens without the beginning they were computed after.
--- choice
Because leaves are always the least recently used nodes.
--- choice
Because the radix tree cannot be rebalanced after removing an inner node.
--- why
KV entries at a position depend on every token before it, so a cached run is only usable together with all its ancestors. Removing leaves keeps every remaining path a valid prefix. And because a request that uses a leaf also uses its ancestors, the ancestors are never colder than their leaves.

+++ question | The hot prompt
--- ask
Seventy percent of a service's requests start with the same long system prompt. A router that always picks the replica with the longest cached match (α = 0) gets a superb hit rate, but its p99 time to first token is far worse than least-loaded routing. Why?
--- choice
Prefix caching makes each prefill slower, because the cached keys must be read.
--- choice correct
Every request with the popular prompt goes to the one replica that cached it first, so that replica queues while the others sit idle.
--- choice
The cache on that replica fills up and starts evicting the popular prompt.
--- choice
Least-loaded routing also caches the prompt on every replica, so it has the same hit rate.
--- why
Pure cache-affinity concentrates load: hits are cheap, but 70% of the traffic on one of several replicas means long queues there. Real routers balance the two, for example by following the cache only while that replica's load stays within a margin of the least loaded.

+++ question | The prefill that remains
--- ask
A 1,200-token prompt arrives at a replica whose cache holds its first 1,000 tokens. In lesson 1's work-list notation, what does its prefill step contain? Type it as a pair, like (5, 10).
--- answer
(200, 1000)
(200,1000)
--- why
200 new tokens go through the model, each attending to the 1,000 cached ones plus the new tokens before it. Compared with (1200, 0), the matrix multiplies shrink six-fold.

+++ practice | How much do prompts share?
--- task
Measure the prefix sharing in a stream of prompts with an unlimited cache. Write `sharing(prompts)`: for each prompt in order, find the length of the longest prefix it shares with **any earlier** prompt (0 for the first). Return a pair `(shared, rate)`: the list of those lengths, and the fraction of all tokens that were shared, `sum(shared) / total tokens`, rounded to 4 decimal places (0.0 if there are no tokens at all).

The starter has the `RadixCache` class from the lesson and the `make_prompts` generator; you may use them (with a capacity larger than all the tokens) or any other way.

For example, `sharing([[1, 2, 3], [1, 2, 4, 5], [1, 9], [1, 2, 4]])` is `([0, 2, 1, 3], 0.5)`.
--- starter


class Node:
    def __init__(self, tokens, parent):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.parent = parent
        self.children = {}
        self.last_used = 0


def shared(a, b):
    n = 0
    while n < len(a) and n < len(b) and a[n] == b[n]:
        n += 1
    return n


class RadixCache:
    """Cached token prefixes in a radix tree, at most `capacity` tokens, evicting least recently used leaves."""

    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.root = Node((), None)
        self.total = 0

    def size(self):
        return self.total

    def match(self, tokens, now=None):
        tokens = tuple(tokens)
        node, i = self.root, 0
        while i < len(tokens) and tokens[i] in node.children:
            child = node.children[tokens[i]]
            n = shared(child.tokens, tokens[i:])
            if now is not None:
                child.last_used = now
            i += n
            if n < len(child.tokens):
                break
            node = child
        return i

    def insert(self, tokens, now=0):
        tokens = tuple(tokens)
        node, i = self.root, 0
        while i < len(tokens):
            child = node.children.get(tokens[i])
            if child is None:
                leaf = Node(tokens[i:], node)
                leaf.last_used = now
                node.children[tokens[i]] = leaf
                self.total += len(tokens) - i
                break
            n = shared(child.tokens, tokens[i:])
            if n < len(child.tokens):
                mid = Node(child.tokens[:n], node)
                node.children[tokens[i]] = mid
                child.tokens = child.tokens[n:]
                child.parent = mid
                mid.children[child.tokens[0]] = child
                child = mid
            child.last_used = now
            node = child
            i += n
        self.evict()

    def path(self, node):
        parts = []
        while node is not self.root:
            parts.append(node.tokens)
            node = node.parent
        return tuple(t for part in reversed(parts) for t in part)

    def evict(self):
        while self.total > self.capacity:
            leaves, stack = [], [self.root]
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                stack.extend(node.children.values())
                if not node.children and node is not self.root:
                    leaves.append(node)
            victim = min(leaves, key=lambda n: (n.last_used, self.path(n)))
            del victim.parent.children[victim.tokens[0]]
            self.total -= len(victim.tokens)


import random


def make_prompts(seed, n, n_system=4, system_len=200, user_len=(20, 80), vocab=1000, weights=None):
    """n chat prompts: one of n_system shared system prompts (chosen with the given weights,
    or uniformly), then the user's own tokens."""
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    systems = [[rng.randrange(vocab) for _ in range(system_len)] for _ in range(n_system)]
    out = []
    for _ in range(n):
        user = [rng.randrange(vocab) for _ in range(rng.randint(*user_len))]
        pick = rng.randrange(n_system) if weights is None else rng.choices(range(n_system), weights)[0]
        out.append(systems[pick] + user)
    return out


def sharing(prompts):
    return ([0] * len(prompts), 0.0)
